president, ladies and gentlemen of the european parliament, freedom of expression in the netherlands is under severe pressure. our party leader geert wilders is due to appear before the dutch judge next week. not because he committed a crime, but because of his political views. the freedom party warns of the consequences of islamization. islam is not a religion, but an ideology that wants to dominate us. islam has nothing in common with western freedom and democracy. criticism of islam is often costly for politicians and opinion makers because of threats from fundamentalists. the fact that politicians who criticize islam in the netherlands are now even pursued by the public prosecutor's office and the courts, and may even end up in prison, is disastrous for our freedom and democracy. we must not allow this to happen and we therefore sound the alarm for the netherlands and the free west. stop the islamization and stop the persecution of politicians because of their political opinion! we will be in the netherlands next wednesday to demonstrate for our freedom. support geert wilders and follow this scandalous political trial against a brave politician: i'm not going to let you down.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, last summer i got elected to the european parliament by voicing criticism against my own government. a young woman in iran, having done the same, would have likely been killed, imprisoned, tortured and raped since. thanks to new media we have all seen the clips of the brutal way the iranian regime cracks down on citizens who speak out peacefully for democracy and freedom. recent arrests of human rights reporters by the intelligence office of the revolutionary guard show that the iranian regime takes an increased interest in closing off the country. their reports were heavily relied upon by international journalists. yesterday was martin luther king day. we commemorate a man who, too, marched the streets peacefully and said: ‘a time comes when silence is betrayal’. high representative, this time has long arrived. president barack obama broke off his holidays over christmas to speak out against yet more brutal attacks on citizens after ashura. in the proposed two-track approach to iran, balancing the nuclear question and human rights, the united states is increasingly emphasising human rights. europe should take a stronger leadership position here, not only when it is political safe to do so. the disaster in haiti is a terrible tragedy, and i am glad you are taking action. however, the man-made disaster that is ongoing in iran has not been met with leadership and coordination from the european side thus far. last month a delegation of this parliament was supposed to visit iran, but the regime did not want us to see with our own eyes its own weakness and division. it is high time that europe takes one stance regarding iran, and the world is waiting. is iran’s current regime, which has lost its legitimacy and is divided internally, a credible actor in negotiations? what measures do you propose in the nuclear question that will target the government without harming the population? are you willing to call an emergency talk in europe on iran? to ensure that human rights will remain europe’s priority, how will you use the eu instrument for human rights? on behalf of the epp group. mr president, during our christmas procession on christmas eve 2009, many churches in iraq, the land of two rivers, remained closed. bomb threats again caused this sad situation, as in the previous year. moreover, even without these threats, churches often remain empty, as more and more christians leave the two-stream country. the violence against them is only increasing, although their numbers are dwindling. three more deadly attacks in mosul before christmas. in addition, christians in the formerly relatively safe kirkuk have been subjected for months to a relentless series of kidnappings and murders, resulting in the flight of hundreds of christian families. iraq had about a million and a half christians in 2003, but now more than half have sought refuge in religious purges, religious purges by islamic extremists. does the european council, does the 27 member state of the european union see this afghanisation of iraq, it is a quote from a christian in iraq, as merely tolerant? i'd like to hear your position, high representative. in any case, a country of the two rivers without its ancient christian congregation will not be able to face a more tolerant future, nor will it be of benefit to europe's security in the long run.
for the greens/efa group today the dutch newspaper reported on the arrest of an asparagus grower on suspicion of trafficking in human beings and the use of slave labour by romanians, european citizens. trafficking in human beings is not only something that happens to citizens from outside the union, but also to citizens from within the union. a well-integrated policy to combat trafficking in human beings cannot be limited to catching such a trafficker and taking firm action against the perpetrators, but must focus on the victims of that crime. their rights and their future must come first. victims of trafficking should never feel that they are alone or that they are abandoned. we must provide them with legal, medical, social, social and financial assistance in all its forms and perhaps also compensation. the fact that they can claim their rights and take advantage of the possibilities in our legal code is crucial in a new directive. i heard mr lópez garrido and mr barrot say very nice things about that. the commissioner also said that people who use the services of trafficked people should be given harsher penalties. i do not think that more severe penalties for such things are wrong, but i do wonder how we are helping the victims if we further criminalise their function, their work - because it is still work even if it is slave labour. how are the victims helped if they have to fear that the work they are doing at the moment will be further criminalised? i'd like an answer to that. victims of human trafficking, if the greens so wish, are also entitled to a residence permit, a permanent residence permit. under certain conditions, to ensure that they do not have to fear being sent back to the country where it all began and to ensure that they can bring charges against the traffickers, while being assured of a safe stay. because there must be no chance, even a small one, that someone will be sent back and there the dealer will run into him again. your new framework directive, commissioner, president of the european union, should be about empowering the victims. this should give them rights and a new future. that's what i'd like to see back.
the presidency wants to speed up accession negotiations, including with iceland. my group is not opposed to this accession, but at the moment there are too many uncertainties about the repayment of the icesave loans to the united kingdom and the netherlands. iceland and icesave have taken great risks for years. the trees seemed to grow to the sky. the borrowing and consuming continued. international bodies, such as the international monetary fund, have warned of these risks. but no, they continued uninterrupted until the shock of the recession. so it is strange that the government in reykjavik is now crying crocodile tears. it was long anticipated that things would go badly wrong. a man with a warning is worth two. in short, iceland can become a member of the eu if it fulfils its international obligations. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the aid is not used to finance the restructuring of the steel industry. only then will there be the necessary confidence for accession.

mr president, i do not want to depict a doom scenario, as has been done here. i do not want to depict a doom scenario for the future, nor do i want to look back in anger about the failure of copenhagen – although i am angry. therefore, the resolution is to do far better in the future, the next opportunity being mexico this year. firstly, do not walk alone next time. in copenhagen, we looked back and we stood alone in our ambitions. we must be able to convince others to be as ambitious as we are. how can we claim to be in the driver’s seat if we fail to convince others of our ambitions and of the destination? secondly, no one size fits all. as sometimes was proudly said during the run-up to the climate summit, there is no plan ‘b’. well, we certainly know it: there is nothing right now. we have nothing. so we had better have a plan ‘b’ next time. last, but not least, how can we win in diversity if our slogan is, i mean, unity. we had better unite next time, and that means one voice. we need a univocal mandate. europe really needs one voice. so, president-in-office, are you willing to commit yourselves to obtaining an exclusive – and not a shared – mandate? it would be an exclusive competence for the european union in climate policy and climate agreements.

author. − mr president, in the week of egyptian orthodox christmas, an attack took place killing and wounding 20 coptic egyptians. although the attack can be seen as a criminal act of individuals, various other troubling incidents require our ongoing focus on respect for all minorities in egypt. violence and hatred cannot be accepted in the name of religion. people have a universal right to freedom of religion as well as freedom from religion. ethnic and religious diversity asks for a vigilant society that is able to reconcile differences in open debate, a society where people of any background or conviction know that their freedoms are guaranteed. an open society can only be realised when the separation of religion and state is implemented in the constitution and throughout the system of government. security measures cannot be the only means to manage a pluralist society. yet state emergency laws have been in place in egypt for the past 28 years. a free debate is perhaps the most powerful medicine against extremism and violence. therefore, freedom of expression, both online and offline, can be seen as the egyptian government’s best tool to resolve tensions in society. it is therefore very difficult to understand or accept that around 30 activists, politicians and bloggers were arrested by government forces while travelling to the southern egyptian town of naga hammadi to express their condolences to the families of those killed during the sectarian violence. the arrests are a particularly striking example of what has become a pattern of the egyptian government’s interference in citizens’ rights to freedom of expression. something is desperately wrong when people are treated like criminals merely for attempting to show sympathy and solidarity with fellow countrymen. in too many cases, the argument of maintaining public order is abused. since 2008, egyptians have been unable to get an unregistered phone line, but the control is not total. new rules are now in force, under which users of wi-fi have to pay for a connection, for which they need to provide an e-mail address to have a password and a username sent. this allows for active control of users by the government. moreover, a draft law is under debate in the parliament about net regulation, providing for prison sentences for ‘publication of multimedia content without government permission’. yet the egyptian constitution says, ‘freedom of expression or freedom of opinion is guaranteed. every individual has the right to express his opinion and to publicise it verbally or in writing or by photography or by other means within the limits of the law’. self-criticism and constructive criticism are the guarantee for the safety of the national structure. i urge the egyptian government to refrain from introducing emergency laws limiting fundamental freedoms in light of the sectarian tensions present. an adequate response to crimes committed in the name of religion is only appropriate. however, this should not be used as a reason to repress the population as a whole, with laws limiting free speech and expression. only when fundamental freedoms are safeguarded by the constitution and all layers of legislation are free will an open society in egypt be possible. the egyptian government should lead its citizens on this road to freedom, and europe should be its strongest partner in this.
the european parliament has been asked to give its opinion on the report by mr glinne, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy. what we see, unfortunately, is that christians are having a particularly hard time in many countries where the islamic faith is dominant. this is the case, for example, in egypt. coptic orthodox christians, but also roman catholic and protestant christians and jews are structurally disadvantaged in egypt. for example, muslims who become christians continue to be oppressed. that's what their ids show. it still says they are muslims, because apostasy is forbidden by law. in particular, the violence against copts has increased enormously in the last ten to twenty years. there have been over 100 attacks, with thousands of victims. i think the egyptian government's position is weak. three men have been arrested, but violence against christians is generally tolerated. i call on the council and the commission to consult egypt immediately so that the government there can take a different path. if cairo does not do so, i think that this must have repercussions for our bilateral relations with egypt.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are not undermined. as a dutch parliamentarian and leader of the freedom party in the dutch parliament, he is being prosecuted for expressing his opinion. geert wilders is being persecuted by the left-wing elite because he warns the netherlands, europe and the world against the fascist ideology called islam. it is an unprecedented scandal. the islamization of the netherlands and europe, mr president, is threatening european judeo-christian and humanistic culture. and where many, including in this parliament, are leaning back and allowing the tsunami of islamization to overwhelm them, the freedom party is fighting for that european culture. president. - the barbaric acts which have taken place in malaysia, egypt and many other places in the world, for example this week in nigeria, are the result of the intolerant fascist ideology known as islam. in muslim countries, non-muslims are systematically humiliated and killed. the events in malaysia and egypt cannot be considered isolated incidents, but have their roots in the ideology that demands respect but does not give it. this parliament can ensure that these terrible events never happen in europe by fighting alongside the freedom party against the islamization of europe. we invite everyone to join us.

on behalf of the gue/ngl group. you said it yourself, we can talk about a lot of animals: the elephant, the asian tiger, the coral. but i want to focus on one animal in particular. yesterday, the rotterdam zoo blijdorp announced that no european zoo has succeeded in breeding polar bears. the european breeding programmes for polar bears are not successful and that is very bad news, especially as the polar bear is threatened with extinction in the wild. as the sea ice shrinks, the polar bear's habitat is slowly disappearing. more than 70% of the polar bear population in the wild could disappear within 45 years. in addition, the polar bear is threatened by trophy hunting and trade. people seem to get a kick out of shooting polar bears for fun, and that's really disgusting to me. i therefore call on the eu to support the proposal to ban commercial trade in polar bears before it is too late; bluefin tuna also has a clear place in annex i.
i could talk about all the species on the agenda for the cites conference at the end of march, but i want to highlight one, precisely because it is such a model of what we are doing as humans right now: the bluefin tuna. this is a beautiful, impressive fish that has been fished for centuries and that we have been eating for centuries. but unfortunately, that fish is dying. after years of mismanagement by politicians who have repeatedly sidelined organic advice, who have allowed short-term economic interests to prevail over the long-term prospects of the sector, we now have no choice but to ban trade in bluefin tuna altogether. there are amendments on the table to do things differently and not to prohibit that trade, but unfortunately it is too late for that. a few weeks ago, a single bluefin tuna was sold for 120,000 euros. that is the reality today. and that explains the huge illegal catches, estimated to be twice the quota. that is why quotas do not provide solace. they're not interested in illegal fishing. the only way to save bluefin tuna is to ban international trade. i know, the consequences for the sector will be severe, i fully realize that. but let us learn from this experience: when the sea is empty, the sector is finished. so let us also be more careful with our nature for economic reasons. i call on the european commission to submit the decision to the council to include bluefin tuna in annex i as soon as possible and i ask the spanish presidency to step over its own shadow and then take that decision. colleagues, in the 17th century, we wiped out the dodo, like humans. let us show our human learning ability and prevent the bluefin tuna from becoming the dodo of the 21st century.
in march, there will be another un summit, this time on trade in endangered species. the opportunity to speak with one voice as the eu once again. science, in particular, has an important role to play in this. let's see what science tells us. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. or if we go for the long term, with the bluefin tuna, we're talking about 2012! that's not long-term, that's tomorrow. therefore, follow the advice of the european parliament and ban the trade in bluefin tuna, but also ban the trade in polar bears and keep the african elephant on the same list to prevent further poaching. finally, there will also be a delegation from the european parliament in doha. i therefore hope that the ep delegation will be able to help define the eu's position so that we can work together to ensure that these species are preserved for our future.

rapporteur. − mr president, i shall start by saying that i, too, support a strong, outward-looking eu that is capable of acting shoulder to shoulder as a true counterpart to the us, and in that framework i think it is of crucial important to be open, fair and transparent if we are to address the issue of how europe should cooperate with the us for counter-terrorism purposes, including law-enforcement use of data collected for commercial ends. without a doubt, the targeted exchange and use of data for counter-terrorism purposes is and will remain necessary, but let me be clear: european citizens must be able to trust both security and data claims. getting it right first time round should be the objective and, with all due respect, the council has been insufficiently strong to do so. indeed, the proposed interim agreement is a significant departure from european law, in how enforcement agencies would obtain financial records of individuals, namely through court-approved warrants or subpoenas to examine specific transactions, but with the proposed interim agreement we instead rely on broad administrative subpoenas for millions of records of european citizens. by the very nature of swift it is not possible to refer to the so-called ‘limited’ requests. for technical reasons swift has to transfer bulk data, thereby violating the basic principles of eu data-protection law such as necessity and proportionality. this cannot be rectified ex post by mechanisms of oversight and control. at all times it must be clear that parliament is not out there to just passively take note of the actions of the council and the commission. the fact is that this house is always being promised jam tomorrow if only we would be patient. however, we cannot keep falling for fake promises of jam tomorrow. we need clear commitments now and the ball was in council’s court, i made that clear last week, but so far it failed to act accordingly. the council states that it wants to ensure the utmost respect for privacy and data, but fails to address in particular the rights of access, rectification, compensation and redress outside the eu for data subjects. the council states that it shares parliament’s concerns and therefore calls on the commission to adopt draft negotiation guidelines. why hide behind the commission? it is council that will adopt the negotiation directives in the end; why have the negotiation guidelines not already been submitted? council states yet again that it wants to ensure that the tftp will continue. however, it fails to address the fact that in this way the eu continues to outsource its financial intelligence service to the us. the lack of reciprocity is not being addressed. true reciprocity would allow the eu authorities to obtain similar data stored in the us and, in the long term, consider the necessity of building up our own eu capacity. the council does not show any commitment to line up with existing legislation such as the data retention directive for the telecom service providers, which does deal with specific and targeted data. the council fails to clarify the precise role of the public authority. a ‘push’ system does not mean anything if in actual practice swift has to transfer bulk data. transfer and storage are, in other words, by definition disproportionate under the terms of the interim agreement and the council does not address a european solution for the supervision of data exchange. president-in- office, tell me how on earth i can tell 500 million european citizens that we are selling out on important safeguards and principles just because we are not able to put our foot down, because council is not able to get its act together. tell me; i am all ears. (applause)

rapporteur. − mr president, a lot has been said. let me underline to mr busuttil and the president-in-office, in particular, that by our withholding our consent on the interim agreement the security of european citizens is not being compromised. i think it is very unfair to use that as a form of argument. other legal instruments remain available for targeted transatlantic data exchange and, as stated by claude moraes, nobody has the moral high ground here. with regard to the usa letter, that letter is obviously appreciated, there is no doubt about that, but to state that parliament’s views will be heard, considered and responded to is a bit weak, is it not? it is very non-committal. also it is stated that this is only provided this interim agreement remains in force, which i consider as pure blackmail. i am getting angry and i am sorry for that, but this debate is slowly getting on my nerves. tftp is not, and cannot be considered to be, consistent with the strongest european traditions on civil liberties. it must be seen, and i want it acknowledged, as a departure from european law and practice. i said this before and i want to hear it again clearly. i also want to say that nobody doubts the necessity for continued and stronger cooperation between the us and the eu, though the eu and its member states, in the council in particular, must be strong in setting their own objectives. in that respect, parliament and the council are not in tune yet. finally, let me stress that it is all about european responsibility, and a european solution is to be found. the netherlands and belgium cannot end up being the ‘dupe’ of all this. i listened carefully to your statements, to the request for more time, and i am willing to refer that request to the conference of presidents, but you did not give me the reassurances i was looking for: it was too vague. i will, however, refer your request to the conference of presidents which will meet later this afternoon.
the european parliament has long ago established the requirements to be met by body scanners if they are to be imported: the commission has also proposed to extend the scope of the directive to cover the following areas: there are techniques now to ensure all of this, so x-rays, x-rays and nude photographs should be obsolete. and that's where things are going wrong right now. at heathrow in london, they use old equipment and there's no freedom of choice. new equipment is being imported into italy. the dutch minister of justice is asking for devices at all airports throughout europe, not just for flights to the us, but for all flights. and if we just continue to have the old-fashioned debate about technology, we fail to ask the real, serious question: i am not sure that this is the way to go. i would like to join my colleague from the social democrats in asking these questions. we must think about what kind of europe we want, because there is no such thing as complete security.
for the ecr group the latest generation of scanners meets the requirements of this parliament. security guards see drawn images of human contours and are indicated by dots when people carry liquids, weapons or other prohibited items. if those spots are visible, those passengers at those spots are checked separately. the latest scanners don't affect health either. the radiation from it is weaker than that from cell phones. besides, these scanners are user-friendly. queues are shortened because more passengers can be checked per hour. so it also saves air passengers money. but, mr president, the latest scanners are additional security tools. profiling and good information exchange are also essential. i think we can move quickly to introducing these instruments as complementary instruments.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial vehicle' to cover vehicles with a maximum mass of less than 100 tonnes. it means that if you're a gambler, you usually lose. gambling is addictive, too. it is a real threat, especially to young people. if some of you have a romantic view of gambling, i have to disappoint you. in essence, it is a multibillion dollar business that is too often associated with criminal activity. in the netherlands, legislation is therefore in place to combat gambling in low-threshold establishments, where many young people come. however, online gambling - often cross-border - makes gambling more accessible once again. in this particular case, we must not assume a free market, but rather encourage member states to take restrictive measures. i think the oral question which has led to our debate is a little too much about market forces. in particular, i am not convinced that it is possible to speak of 'responsible gambling' via the internet. as far as i'm concerned, online gambling is restricted as much as possible. the court of justice has recognised that member states must be able to intervene and i therefore call on the commission not to make any attempt to reduce the level of protection by means of proposals for european regulations, but rather to encourage member states to regulate a good level of protection and also not to challenge member states before the court, as mrs gebhardt said, but rather to enter into a dialogue on the best possible protection.
mr president, the netherlands has about 120,000 gambling addicts. that's almost 1% of our population. gambling addiction leads to serious social problems, such as broken families, money problems, and crime. member states should therefore make every effort to combat gambling and its associated problems to the maximum extent possible. it is striking, mr president, that some member states have a completely legal gambling market. in addition, the gambling industry wants us to believe that the gambling market is a normal part of the internal market and that therefore no restrictions need to be imposed on it. i love that. member states should not facilitate markets that promote social misery. sadly, many are unable to resist the temptation to gamble. the dutch authorities have therefore decided to take control of the gambling market and to allow only a state monopoly on gambling. although i would prefer not to see gambling palaces being built anywhere in the european union, i still consider this, mr president, to be the least bad solution. mr president, we as the european parliament must call very strongly on the member states which allow gambling to discourage that market wherever possible.

continue to maintain monopolies on the basis of the european court of justice's judgment that 'if you have a restrictive policy, you can have a monopoly', but it is totally out of control. and that is rapporteur. − mr president, commissioners, dear colleagues – also those who are leaving the room right now – this report was adopted by a large majority in the committee on economic and monetary affairs (econ) and it is the fruit of a joint effort of all political groups. i would like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs – who are still present as far as i can see – for their excellent cooperation. econ welcomes in particular the emphasis on consumers. the previous commissioner, mrs kroes, did a splendid job of making the consumer the focus of the competition policies, and we trust commissioner almunia will build on that. that brings me to the first big issue, namely that of cartels. we discussed at great length issues like the most effective sanctions, the fairness of high fines and the feasibility of criminal sanctions. however, before i enter into the details of our discussions, i would like to remind european companies that the best guarantee for escaping sanctions is by simply not engaging in cartels. you may think that you are outsmarting the competition authorities but, in reality, you are doing harm to the consumer. cartels are not smart. they are reprehensible. we therefore welcome the firm stance taken by the european commission on anti-competitive behaviour. it is essential that sanctions punish bad behaviour, in particular of repeat offenders, but they must encourage compliance at the same time. sanctions must have a sufficiently deterrent effect. high fines are an effective tool but, as a single instrument, they may be too blunt. therefore we invite the commission to come up with proposals to make the toolkit more sophisticated and more effective. in the report we suggest looking at issues such as individual responsibility, transparency and accountability of firms, shorter procedures, due process and the development of european standards and corporate compliance programmes. a second key issue is state aid. in the context of the economic crisis huge amounts of state aid have been granted. exceptional circumstances call for exceptional measures. we recognise that, but we should not lose sight of the fact that granting state aid comes at a price. it distorts competition and it leads to record levels of public debt and budget deficits. the bill for this episode will be presented to future generations. we have a duty to justify every single cent that has been spent. i am therefore pleased that econ urges the commission to do a thorough evaluation of the results of the exceptional state aid operation. we would in particular like to see an evaluation of state aid granted for the so-called green recovery. two years ago we were persuaded to accept the recovery package and the state aid measures with the promise that it would be used to bring about the long overdue shift to a sustainable knowledge economy. now we ask you, has the money indeed brought about that shift? what has it been spent on? who were the beneficiaries and what have they actually done for green recovery? we also need clarity, commissioner, about the impact of the state aid exercise in the financial sector and, in particular, its possible distortive effects. i would like now to say a few words on the issue of vertical restraints. we know the current agreement will be revised for may of this year. the european commission previously committed to closely involving the european parliament in the review process. however, to my disappointment, i had to read about the latest version of the proposals in the media. when i then asked the commission to get the same documents as had been leaked to the press, it took considerable arm-twisting to get them and i cannot hide my annoyance at this. the commission should put an end to the systematic and deliberate leaking to the press. to deny that it happens, frankly, is an insult to our intelligence. on substance, the commission proposals allow for discrimination against online retailers who do not have a bricks-and-mortar shop. i have used my prerogative as a rapporteur for tabling an amendment asking the commission to correct this situation. in the 21st century we must encourage, not stifle, competition from online retailers. we call on the commission to put consumer interests over vested interests. we ask the commission to carry out the long overdue sector inquiries, in particular into online advertising, which this house has requested several times. if the commission does not wish to do so, we would like to understand the criteria for refusing. finally, commissioner, we very much welcome commissioner almunia’s pledge to closely involve parliament in shaping competition policies. the economic crisis has clearly shown the need - i'm the reporter. - mr president, there is a lot to be said about european transport policy. i'm not going to do that, i'm going to concentrate on a few key points and a few main lines. firstly, there is the interest of the sector. 80% of world trade is carried by sea and the european fleet has a 41% share of that global market. within the european market, 40% of all goods are transported by sea. so this is a crucial economic sector which is facing global competition. when you realize that, the second spearhead, competition, actually follows. we know that many countries around the world support their navies in a variety of ways. if we leave that to europe, in a few years all our ships will be sailing to hong kong or singapore. that would not only be detrimental to the fleet but, more importantly, the entire maritime cluster would be hit. employment on shore, in banks, shipyards, insurance companies, logistics companies, training and educational institutions, companies active in the field of innovation and improvement of environmental performance would be dealt an insurmountable blow by the departure of our fleet. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. it is about providing fiscal facilities, such as a tonnage tax system for ships and tax advantages for seafarers and shipowners. this is the only way in which the maritime sector and clusters can remain relevant in europe in the long term. i have a question for the commissioner. when will the european commission come up with rules on state aid to seaports? that proposal was supposed to come in the autumn, but i don't know what year that autumn is. i am interested in transparency and accountability in the way aid is provided. we should not start supporting terminals which are not in operation, nor should we start supporting ports which are geographically very close to third countries' ports. in either case, that is giving vitamin supplements to a dead horse. in this context, i would like to make a comment on paragraph 5 of the motion for a resolution, which refers to 'flags of convenience' in the english text. i think that is a misunderstanding and i would have preferred to have read the only correct term here, namely 'substandard' ships. what we do not want are flags and ships which evade the minimum standards of safety and social standards. it is a question of the quality of the flag, and the so-called cheap flag is certainly not automatically a flag of convenience. finally, an important point of reference, namely to make the maritime sector attractive to young people. europe is ageing fast and needs to do more for its young people. the information on the maritime sector and on shipping cannot be started too early, and i was pleased to hear that shipowners' organisations are already working in primary schools to educate young people about working at sea. so far in this first round, a few points from me and i shall be happy to go over what the colleagues and the commissioner have to say when i have the floor.
member of the commission. − mr president, this has been a fascinating debate and definitely strengthens the excellent report by mr van dalen. i think it is well done because there is nothing more difficult than to write a report on strategy in general, because strategy is an instrument which really has a longer-term vision and at the same time it needs to concentrate on main points. we are working now in the college to prepare a detailed road map for implementation. by the summer we could come out with this. i should like to refer to three specific questions. one concerns state aid guidelines for ports. this is under discussion in the commission’s dg on competition and with a former commissioner for competition and they really would like us, as a commission, to work in line together, so it will take some time. i cannot give any precise time lines but we are working on it. on the marco polo issue, we have discussed the issues relating to the trans-european transport network, so this discussion will be related to this. on maritime ports, we do not have any legislation at this stage in the pipeline. finally, thank you once again, mr president and honourable members, for this debate. it was really very helpful for the commission.

vice-president of the commission. − mr president, i am very happy to present to parliament the report on the action taken by the commission regarding the effects of the european airspace crisis as a result of the volcanic eruption of eyjafjallajökull. the house will know that 84 000 flights have been cancelled, affecting thousands of passengers. as you know, eurocontrol, at our initiative, convened a telephone conference on monday morning and the commission participated actively yesterday afternoon in an extraordinary council of transport ministers. there are, in my view, four key messages to convey following the council. all the transport ministers are in favour of a coordinated european response to the crisis. national solutions are not effective in dealing with these kinds of problems, which affect airspace globally. i must underline that the spirit of cooperation amongst the transport ministers was high and we have had several telephone conversations where everybody stated they were ready to take responsibility and cooperate. the second important point is that safety comes first. there cannot be any compromise on safety. this is, and remains, our fundamental concern. we have to ensure the highest safety standards for our citizens. the third principle is that the ministers agreed to the progressive and coordinated opening of european airspace while ensuring safety. this started this morning at 8.00, through eurocontrol. three types of zones are included in this decision, based on the degree of contamination. the first of these zones is located at the nucleus of the emissions, where total restriction of operations will be maintained, given that it is impossible to guarantee its safety. the second of the zones is one which will not, in principle, impede air traffic cooperation being carried out, even though ash is still present. that zone will need to be confirmed, and the decisions on operations will be taken in a coordinated manner by the authorities in the member states. the third zone is not affected by ash, resulting in no restrictions of any type on operations. eurocontrol is providing maps every six hours with the relevant information for the national authorities. fourth, through these measures we are ultimately anticipating the implementation of the single european sky programme and, in particular, the functions of the network manager. i know i can count on the strong support of parliament following the success of the second single european sky package last year. you will know, and the ministers mentioned, that a taskforce – a group of commissioners – has been organised to discuss state aid issues. yesterday i talked with airline representatives and they said that they are simply not ready yet to assess their losses. the main question for them, owing to all the economic consequences, is to resume flights. the model for resuming flights is the most important thing. we should not panic about state aid and any other measures to help the air transport sector. passenger rights is another important issue, and we must enforce the implementation of passenger rights. the rules are good ones. everyone shares the view that the rules are good. the question is enforcement, which again is in the hands of the member states. we should really push ahead with that and have several ideas on how to better enforce the rules. i would now like to comment on what are, in my view, deliberate attempts to confuse things – who should do what, who has done what and what the models are. it is clear that in some countries elections are coming up, and so on, but after the volcanic eruption all the decisions made were based on existing and agreed models on how to handle these kinds of situation. this model is intergovernmental and airspace is a national competence. it is not the commission which is giving the orders – there are rules covering our national systems and our model, to repeat, is based on existing information and existing assessments. there is nothing wrong with that model. we can now think about how to modify the model. we started discussing this yesterday. to say that the european model completely failed is totally wrong. this was, and is, an extraordinary event. the eruption of such a volcano and the unexpected widespread cloud of ash is something which has happened on very rare occasions in the world – it is not like snow or the like, which occurs often. it was already clear at the weekend that the situation was turning into something very exceptional, and over the weekend we had several discussions on how to approach the issue. to say that the transport ministers should have intervened immediately is completely contrary to our understanding of how things are organised in europe. those kinds of decisions are in the hands of independent experts and independent bodies. myself and minister lópez garrido were at eurocontrol on sunday, and i was in contact with all the transport ministers of the larger member states. we were ready to take responsibility and ask what we should do to resolve the situation. however, these cannot be arbitrary decisions, but are in the hands of a special body. that body held a meeting, and we held discussions with it, on sunday. these were very difficult discussions, as what was under consideration were people’s lives. on monday morning there was an extraordinary eurocontrol council, which agreed on the so-called ‘free zones’ model. we were very happy that eurocontrol was cooperative. to repeat, this was not within the community’s competence at all, but events have shown that a national approach was an obsolete approach. we definitely have a greater impetus now to create a more european approach to these kinds of events and to regulate them. we must also, of course, assess the consequences and outcome. the main thing – as everybody, including the airlines, said – was that it was very important to resume flights. concerning passengers, the most important thing is to get people back home or to their destinations. that was the key issue addressed yesterday. to sum up, we are working intensively with the council and with eurocontrol to monitor the situation, and if necessary to take new decisions. the model provided for now is quite suitable for resuming the vast majority of flights.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the safety of aircraft. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that is why it is important that we have this urgent debate in this parliament today. passengers are stuck, airlines, the travel industry, businesses that depend on air travel, all have been hit hard. and that's on top of the economic crisis. safety is of the utmost importance. we must not allow any misunderstanding about that. passengers must be transported safely, but it's clear we were not properly prepared for this extraordinary situation. the closure of airspace on the first day was a quick response to a problem unknown in europe, the ash cloud. but what did we see in the days after? the computer models showed that no flights were allowed, but test flights went smoothly. of course, once again, safety is paramount, but it is important that the opening up of european airspace be based on facts and real principles. more tailor-made work is needed and it is good that the first steps were taken yesterday and that it must be built up quickly. the ability to fight. safety first, but safe zones must also be able to be used again quickly. of course, structural measures are also needed. the single european sky, which is so much resisted by the member states, can also help us to make aviation much more efficient. airline companies have suffered great economic damage. the closure of air traffic, but also the cost of the assistance to be provided to stranded passengers, was high. insurance does not cover anything and it is questionable whether it is justified to put all the costs on the airlines. i therefore call on you to examine quickly what costs have been incurred, what damage has been suffered and where compensation may be available. let me give you an example. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. is it not obvious that we should consider whether we can afford to do so from the european budget in this situation of overwhelming power? commissioner almunia, you have indicated that you are sympathetic to state aid, but i want to warn you, because we must prevent member states from sponsoring their national champions. that is why it is extremely important that it be coordinated at european level. not only the state aid framework, but also the actual granting of state aid. i would like to call you to that.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the transport sector is not affected by the effects of climate change. a not-so-large volcano in iceland is erupting, and air traffic has been grounded in large parts of europe for days. i think it is right that we have at least reached a partial resumption of the cancelled flights today. i think that is also justified and can be done, because test flights have shown that it can be flown, especially under the current good visibility conditions. at the same time, i think we have been too rigid to shut down air traffic so completely in one fell swoop. a comparison has been made with the klm flight that crashed into the volcanic dust of mount redoubt over alaska in 1989. it is also quick to refer to the british airways flight over indonesia which hit volcanic dust in 1982. remember, both those flights were completely buried in the dust of recently erupted volcanoes and relatively close to them. the density and heat of the dust particles in those flights cannot be compared to the current situation. i therefore welcome the approach which takes account of the differences in the concentration of the volcanic dust. if you take that approach - and it will happen - then it is right that we should open up some of the airspace already, especially on certain corridors and at certain altitudes. i think that opening up is also very necessary, because the icelandic ash is choking cash from our airlines. i don't mind that this crisis may cause a few weak brothers in aviation to fall. but it must not be that large and reputable companies that put safety first would also fail. it's a lot of money and a lot of jobs. then we have to work with a realistic approach that takes into account the concentration of the dust particles. the airspace can rightly be reopened today for certain parts and that pragmatic approach must also apply in the future so that a good and, above all, safe and responsible balance between safety and economy is achieved.

transfers between third countries and it would demand prior identification of a specific bank whereas the tfcp is based on searches for fund transfers. it is therefore crucial, and i would like to stress that, it is therefore crucial that negotiations focus on finding a solution to make one compatible with the other. we can, of course, insist on a redesign of the tftp but honestly in the end this is not in our hands really and i can therefore only urge the council and the commission, like mr busuttil did, to address the fundamental policy decisions straight away. i do expect a clear and binding commitment on the part of both the council and the commission to undertake all that is necessary to effectively introduce a durable, legally sound, european solution to the extraction of data on european soil. let me stress once more that the transfer and storage of data in bulk to a foreign power, even if it concerns our best friends, is and remains by definition, disproportionate. it marks a huge departure from eu legislation and practice. the rule of law is crucially important and in this context parliament must be extremely mindful in assessing envisaged agreements such as the one we are discussing today. i do support, like others, a strong outwards-looking eu that is capable of acting shoulder to shoulder, as an equal partner, with the us. in that framework i can only underline once more that it is the eu that needs to lay down the principles on how europe will cooperate with the us for counterterrorism purposes, including law enforcement and the use of data collected for commercial ends. getting it right should be the objective, and the european legal demands for the fair, proportionate and lawful processing of personal information are of paramount importance and must always be upheld. now it is for the council and the commission to put this into concrete action as soon as possible and to negotiate an agreement that meets all of the eu and us expectations.
president. - i am sorry, mr president, but i have to say that i am not in favour of the motion for a resolution. this parliament is concerned both with fundamental rights and the protection of the privacy of citizens and with data protection. these are fundamental rights, and with fundamental rights you don't make a simple cost-benefit analysis. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. i do not believe that any of this is not technically possible and i think it is more a matter of money and cost. as i said, when it comes to fundamental rights, it's not simply about how much it costs. it is also important that europe should present itself as an equal negotiating partner, not one that simply gives cups or waits for the us to set the rules. parliament has given the council and the commission the power and the scope to play this role seriously now, and i would ask you, commission and council, to take account of the european convention on human rights, which is now in force. we shall also have to see that reflected in your mandate and in the outcome of your negotiations. i hope you come back with the right thing, i hope you use the power and authority that we gave you last time, and i look forward to seeing what you come back with.

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, as rapporteur i would first of all like to thank the shadows for their excellent, pleasant and fruitful cooperation resulting in a joint resolution. of course this is not the final stage, as our talks on the topic and the text will continue. today we consider the request for consent by the council on the two agreements with the us and australia. this house has always been highly critical of the use and transfer of pnr data. as a matter of fact, in 2004 parliament sought the annulment of the agreement with the us before the european court of justice. it would therefore be inconsistent with our earlier positions to give consent without further ado. however, parliament being responsible and cooperative as always, we agree that by rejecting the two agreements we will create legal uncertainty and practical difficulties for citizens and carriers. so we propose instead to suspend the vote and request the commission to develop a coherent approach to the use of pnr that is based on a single set of principles. i am very pleased to note that the commission and the council have embraced this strategy and that they are committed to working fast and flexibly. we urge the commission in particular to submit the pnr package, as it is now dubbed, before the summer break. such a coherent single approach seems the pragmatic option when more and more countries are requiring the transfer of passenger data. then there is the lapsed pnr agreement with canada – or whatever the legal status is, as this is not entirely clear – as well as the shelved proposal for an eu pnr. this draft resolution sets out a number of basic principles and minimum requirements for the pnr package, and they are, as it were, our conditions for consent. a key element or key word here is proportionality, because it must be demonstrated convincingly that the same end cannot be achieved with less intrusive means. this is really the key to everything. we specifically need to look at api data and esta in this context. we need, for example, to distinguish very clearly between the massive collection and use of data on all passengers for the purpose of automated searches such as profiling and data mining on the one hand, and targeted searches for known suspects on the other hand, identifying people who are, for example, on a no-fly or watch list. that is something completely different and we need to distinguish very carefully. secondly, there must be a clear and strict purpose limitation in line with earlier resolutions, and we insist that data be used only for law-enforcement and security purposes and on the basis of very precise definitions of what that is: organised international crime and international terrorism. we need to make very clear what it is that we are talking about. any use of pnr must be in line with eu data protection standards. it is our prime responsibility to represent the interests of our own european citizens. they have a right to know that we uphold european law in international relations and in our internal policies. finally, we recognise the need to provide law-enforcement and security authorities with the necessary means to do their job in an era of unprecedented mobility, but europe also has a duty to protect our rights and freedoms. i believe with the forthcoming pnr package we have a unique opportunity to get it right.
the tension is a little bit off, ladies and gentlemen. mrs int't veld has drawn up a wonderful resolution which is being embraced by the commission and the council. that's very good in itself. i agree with her, too. i think it's very wise to say now: we are making one clear directive for all pnr contracts for the future in which we take proportionality into account, we only include data which are strictly necessary for the purpose, namely the fight against terrorism, and we also make it clear that this is the purpose and that there is no other purpose. it must ensure that there is reciprocity, that data is not stored for long periods of time, that there is a real end time and that we respect our fundamental rights. the european convention on human rights has now been declared binding, so that will also have to be reflected in such a pnr convention. i therefore think that it is the right time to submit this to the european union fundamental rights office in vienna. i would ask the commission to do the same. the other thing we should be aware of is that in the meantime the habit has developed of citizens actually having their conversation with a foreign power, in this case often the united states, through a company, namely an airline, which has nothing to do with it, which should not actually have certain data from me and which should not want to play this mediating role. we have to do something about that.

on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, our s&d group joins commissioner rehn in expressing our condolences after the death of the nigerian president, umaru yar’adua. the violence between the muslim and christian population in jos, nigeria, in january and march this year, shows the tense and explosive state of this region. although the obvious reason seems to lie in the religious dimension, we must also focus on other underlying causes, as my colleague gay mitchell rightly pointed out as well. most importantly, the region suffers from scarce resources and the unequal access of different groups to these resources. also, the struggle for fertile farmland is an important root cause for the violent conflicts between christians and muslim settlers. indigenous farmers feel threatened by settlers looking for pasture for their livestock. we therefore call for a wider examination of the causes of this conflict. if nothing is done about the poverty and discrimination, these clashes will continue. this means that the whole population needs equal opportunities and equal access to essential goods such as proper education or access to political power. a long-term and lasting solution can only be found if all these factors are taken into account. we call for a fair and transparent prosecution of the perpetrators of the violence, but we are shocked to hear that local governors are now threatening to execute death row inmates only in order to ease the overcrowding in nigerian prisons, where people have to wait for years before they even see a judge. the nigerian state governors should do better by addressing the many underlying problems in the criminal justice system. only then can the perpetrators of the violent clashes have a fair and transparent trial.

the commission is looking into the best way of serving consumers when it is right. we have made a number of proposals. if a toy is made of more than 85% textile, we think it should simply comply with this. and then some people say, yes, but we already have a directive on the safety of toys. but it is specifically about safety and not about informing the consumer about what that product is made of; i imagine the consumer wants to know. there is also an amendment which reads as follows: the consumer has the right to know whether a product is made from animal materials, no further research is required, the producer must indicate whether an animal product has been incorporated. we're not talking about the fiber, we're talking about other materials, like pieces of fur. finally, we have the famous made in italy for which the commission already made proposals in 2005. now, we have actually copied that literally and we hope that with the support of the commission and parliament the council will go over and take a positive view of things. i therefore call on the council to reach a compromise with the commission and parliament which will lead to an improvement of this regulation for the benefit of consumers and to better information.

the problems of greece and the eurozone are a serious situation. that calls for a structural approach. the proposed europe 2020 strategy, which aims to contribute to economic growth and employment, seeks to provide for this. the strategy rightly includes sound public finances. however, the initiative will only be useful if two conditions are met. firstly, the operation of the stability and growth pact needs to be strengthened so that such a situation does not reoccur. it's not just about structure and rules. it's about the mindset. the key is that the member states respect their budgetary commitments. i also drew attention to this at a conference in athens last week. it is good that the member states should be calling on each other more intensively in the council to bring about the necessary structural reforms to put public finances back on track. i think that this is what strengthening economic coordination should be about. the second condition i would like to stress is that it is not desirable for the european union to focus on the policy areas of social affairs, employment and social cohesion. objectives in these areas are rightly not enforceable at european level. we have seen in the lisbon strategy that such an approach does not work. the same applies here: the european community is a great example of this.
today we are talking about the long-term economy of europe and how we can achieve sustainable growth there. and when europe talks about it, i sleep a little less well, because so far europe has not proposed much. i would remind you of the lisbon strategy, in which europe agreed to become the most competitive economy in the world. that, of course, has proved a complete failure. the european union has only created more bureaucracy, unnecessary rules, especially in my country, the netherlands, tax increases, mass immigration, crime and insecurity. and now let us look at the list of countries that want to join, which we sit at the table with every day: albania, bosnia, turkey, all poor countries and islamic countries, corrupt and criminal, iceland a bankrupt country! and every day we hear in this house from the greens and the socialists, and even from the liberals, that we must have a green economy, that we must subsidize billions in wind turbines and green electricity, while our industry is becoming less and less competitive with china, america and india. do you think it's funny that our economy is stagnating? there is only one recipe for an economically strong europe, and that is less bureaucracy and officials in brussels and also less bureaucracy in the member states. tax cuts instead of increases. and stop this mass immigration. greece must of course be thrown out of the eurozone. let us hold up a mirror and look at our colleagues here: what have you done in recent years to ensure that greece has kept its commitments under the stability and growth pact? i already know the answer: not a word! you have all been sleeping here while countries like greece, which receive billions from europe every year, have been squandering that money and growing their government out of their clutches. if europe fails to do so, i predict that my country, the netherlands, and germany will leave the euro!
in the present debate on this crisis we are mainly talking about budgetary discipline. and rightly so, budget discipline is important. but that's not the whole story. let us also keep this crisis in perspective. and the perspective is that we're still talking about a banking crisis, actually. for years, banks with incomprehensible structures have made money on the basis of air. and this bubble burst in 2008. then countries converted that private debt into public debt. and that is the problem facing greece today, a public debt that it cannot bear. in this sense, when we talk about a strategy for 2020, we must also look at the role of banks in it. the commission is completely lacking in ambition in these areas. there's hardly any mention of the benches. what this crisis shows is that a clear distinction must be made between savings banks and investment banks. where is the commission's ambition for plans to tackle this? that's one point. but we must also look to the economy of the future. the economy of the future will be efficient in its use of natural resources. the commission's ambition is again lacking. the commission proposes either vague targets, or targets that are too low, for example 20% less greenhouse gas emissions, which is not enough to stimulate green innovation. how do companies get signals to invest in green technology? we are also missing that in the commission's plans. finally, we have our own budget. it also has to be in line with our own strategy. this means that the structural funds must ultimately be about encouraging new green technology. at the moment, we are mainly subsidizing more greenhouse gas emissions. where is the money for innovation and where is the agricultural budget for sustainable agriculture? we need concrete action and ambition from the commission and not vague plans, which are not going to save us from this crisis.
mr president, as we seek to solve the financial and economic crisis, i would like to point out an essential deficit: that of knowledge. i regret to say that europe is becoming a more stupid continent every day, even though knowledge is our most fruitful and rich resource. investing in it bears no risk. the young generation of europeans pays the price of the crisis, however, as youth unemployment grows and education and innovation budgets are frozen or cut. we have to cut budgets, but let us do so with a 21st century attitude, because where is half of the eu budget going now? to agriculture! it should go to the young generation, and their development. not just a traditional education but one that focuses on e-skills and entrepreneurship for example. we know that the higher one’s education is, the lower the chances of losing a job. yet member states are not meeting the necessary commitment in fostering an ambitious knowledge economy. europe thus punishes the next generation of europeans by not allowing them to develop their talents and ambitions to their full capacity, and it allows china, india and the united states to become more attractive places for talent, research, creativity and innovation. short-term measures will have a high long-term cost. entrepreneurship, excellence and a sustainable future of the european economy can only be fostered if we are willing to invest in knowledge. because whoever thinks knowledge is expensive does not know what stupidity costs. today commissioner kroes presented her vision for europe’s digital agenda, one of the eu 2020 flagship programmes. it is an ambitious yet concrete strategy which seeks to make europe’s digital market stronger in a global economy as well as connecting many europeans to an open internet. i believe we need to take more smart decisions now and make sure the current crisis does not become a mortgage to the youth and the young generation charging an interest that they can never pay back. does the commission support a coordinated approach, moving away from the intergovernmental procedure in decision-making, and giving a stronger role to the commission and parliament, to ensure that member states fulfil their commitments?

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, blasphemy laws in pakistan, as various colleagues have pointed out, are easily abused by extremists as an excuse to use violence against religious or non-religious minorities. of course freedom of religion is important, but freedom from religion is also important. these blasphemy laws in a society in which freedom of expression is repressed in the name of religion pose another risk; last wednesday a court in pakistan banned the social networking site facebook in the country. the pakistani telecom authority instructed all internet providers to block the website. the measure was taken to prevent people from learning about alleged insulting remarks on islam or the prophet mohammed. a cartoonist took the initiative to call on people to produce drawings of the prophet to resist pressures such as that brought upon the popular series south park, which led to edits in the programme. facebook as well as on-line services and platforms are an important virtual passage to the rest of the world. it opens up sources of information and contact for pakistanis and allows them to engage in an exchange of ideas; especially the young generation of pakistanis can be enlightened through the internet, as school materials often contain discriminatory and one-sided language. criminalising free speech is not an effective way of allowing pakistani society to deal with diversity. cartoonists, journalists and citizens should be able to speak their minds freely even if that means insulting some people. challenges to free speech are not exclusively found in pakistan; in europe we face serious challenges to open debate as well. threats to journalists, cartoonists and artists are becoming more common and they challenge our liberal, democratic societies. self-censorship is becoming more common and politicians, cartoonists and journalists now need protection against death threats. let us be inspired to stand for freedom of expression in the european union but also in pakistan and the world over. it is the best remedy against extremism.
for the epp group at the founding of the state of pakistan in 1947, its founder, muhammad ali jinnah, uttered the memorable words: "we start with the fundamental principle that we are all citizens with equal rights". today, especially for the estimated 15 million christians in pakistan, a widespread atmosphere of intimidation and threats surrounds them, both in the cities and in the countryside as far as the capital islamabad. sadly, the pakistani legal system perpetuates the precarious situation of indigenous christians. the result of a historical process of the gradual islamization of pakistani society since the late 1970s, with the resultant alarming deterioration of the legal position of the pakistani christian community. specifically, we are talking about the witness and blasphemy laws of the 1980s, legal instruments that effectively exempt christians. for the testimony of a non-muslim in a court of law is worth only half the testimony of a muslim if the judge wishes to hear all the testimony of a christian! even more dangerous are the blasphemy laws for pakistani christians. they impose life imprisonment for desecration of the koran and the death penalty for disparaging remarks about islam and the prophet muhammad. in short, on the testimony of a random muslim, the life of a christian in pakistan can suddenly end in a death cell! this, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, creates an almost unbearable atmosphere of fear and uncertainty. hundreds of pakistani christians languish in prisons for years without any trial. i therefore urge the council and the commission to link all forms of assistance to the pakistani authorities to the urgent need to end the deadly discrimination against the country's religious minorities.
author. − madam president, thailand is going through a violent crisis with the red-shirt demonstrators on the one hand, and the army and the yellow-shirts on the other. around 80 people have already lost their lives and nearly 2 000 have been injured. democracy is threatened and the country finds itself in a government-declared state of emergency. the state of emergency has resulted in censorship. media sources such as tv, radio and internet have been blocked. halting violence is very important, and we encourage all parties to use self-restraint in the process of doing so, but in seeking to restore the calm the state of emergency should not be abused to restrict fundamental rights and individual freedoms. censorship needs to end and freedom of the media and expression need to be restored. these values are important for the european parliament in their valuable relationship with thailand.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development. − mr president, i am moved by your proverbial patience. thank you very much and my humble apologies. the current economic crisis has hit the developing countries hard – and harder than the countries in the developed world – even though they are not responsible for the causes of this crisis. the same applies to climate change: developing countries have little to do with the causes of climate change, but they are suffering the most from its effects. it is therefore of great importance that the eu makes sure that money meant for development goals actually serves this purpose and this purpose alone. first of all, it means that the budget aimed at supporting developing countries in their fight against the effects of climate change should be additional money. the ‘fast-start’ climate finance package should not come at the expense of existing development cooperation programmes. secondly, i support the principle of financial assistance to the main acp banana-producing countries. however, this should not be financed via the margins, and i strongly oppose the draft proposal by the commission in which funds from the development cooperation instruments are redeployed for this assistance. funds used under the dci must target poverty alleviation, and although i support the creation of an instrument for cooperation with industrialised countries – ici+ – that instrument should not be financed by the dci either.
the european union is a major player in the world economy. i would have liked to have seen mr de gucht, the trade commissioner, sitting next to you, because we are trying to catch up in this house over the next five years. the millennium development goals are not on the agenda, but we all signed them, we all promised to do so. we promised - and i remember that slogan, because i worked for development agencies at the time - "end poverty in the world", or in english "make poverty history". we're still not making history here. and it can. we can talk about 0.7% of our gross national product in aid and even more. we can talk about new forms of financial aid, but that is becoming more and more complicated in these times of economic crisis. but what we really need to talk about is a different way of living together, about fair trade. we may be complaining about being the world's largest provider of development aid, but we should be complaining about being the world's largest consumer market. if we were to act honestly, a lot of help would probably not be needed. somehow it remains easier to give aid than to say "we will no longer compete with small farmers in africa". if we restrict the ability of multinationals to evade taxes, there are a lot of coffers in africa that would be better filled. and when i see how we deal with the voting lists for this resolution, where there is a lot of pushing and shoving, i wonder where the big gesture is. where is our signal that in five years we will really be meeting the millennium development goals?
president. - there are members who claim to be serving the interests of selfemployed drivers if they adhere to the 48-hour working week. these members claim that road safety is served. that is a very nasty campaign, because the selfemployed drivers will continue to be covered by the driving and resting time regulations. there is no risk to road safety. the problem is that some southern european trade unions fear that self-employed romanian and bulgarian drivers will take over the driving. these members are running like trembling lap dogs after the unions. that's the whole point. transport commissioner kallas wants to keep the self-employed driver out of the directive. he deserves our support in the votes tomorrow. if that fails, 16 june will be a black day in the history of the european parliament. the citizen will then be left with a staircase and piles of bureaucracy. and then complain that the next election will be even lower.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his statement at the beginning of this debate and i would also like to thank mrs sommer for her work as rapporteur. i have the impression, having been in this parliament for almost a year, that we think that with simple legislation we can change the behaviour of millions of europeans for a moment. that is the same idea we have in this regulation. we have to realize that nothing is as difficult as changing people's behavior, especially when it involves 500 million people. so the influence that we're ultimately going to have on that behavior through labeling is going to be limited. that does not mean that i am completely dismissing this regulation, not at all. but we must be realistic about the power of this instrument. we must realize that consumers have a right to information. and whatever the outcome of this debate, consumers will have much more information at the end of the second reading. in the long run, however, we need to invest more in education and educate people from the bottom up to lead healthy lifestyles. another point is that the debate in this parliament is about the fundamental aims of this directive. is it forcing people to make healthy food choices or is it giving people the choice to determine for themselves what food they choose? we liberals believe that we should give the people the information and that the people have the power to make the choices themselves. finally, we need to find a balance in the amount of information we give people. too much will never work, and too little will never work. i think we're on the right track on our own. let's trust that the people who can vote for us also have enough power to choose the right food in the store, if they're given the right information.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: tomorrow you will confess. do you support a clear colour coding system for food labels or do you rely on industry? the political choice is simple as far as i am concerned. if you want to protect commercial interests, you support the ideas of the food industry. if you want a label that helps you choose for yourself whether it's high, medium or low in sugar, salt or fat, vote for a color-coding system. 60% of your constituents are overweight. 25% of our children are overweight. 25% of people have reading problems. why is it that everyone finds color codes on, say, energy labels for homes, cars, or electronics normal, but it's peculiar on food? nutritionists and consumer organizations recommend simple color coding so that food labels are understandable and you can make an easy choice for yourself. i support that, too.
