in writing. − i support all efforts to make rail freight more effective, because of its environmental advantages compared to road freight. however, i want to make it clear that i oppose the ongoing liberalisation of railways in the european union, which is responsible for the fragmentation and ineffectiveness of rail freight today, as well as the lack of investment in this area. this compromise with the council is an effort to improve the situation. therefore i am voting in favour, even though it builds on the liberalisation that i strongly oppose. this vote does not concern deregulation, which is already provided for in the treaty.
in writing. − explanation of vote on marinescu (a7-0162/2010). i support efforts to make rail freight more effective, because of its environmental advantages compared to road freight. however, i want to make it clear that i oppose the ongoing liberalisation of railways in the european union, which is responsible for the fragmentation, lack of investment and ineffectiveness of rail freight today. this compromise with the council is an effort to improve the situation, so i voted for it, but it builds on the liberalisation that i strongly oppose. this vote does not however concern deregulation, which is already in the treaty.

mr president, since the time of tsar peter the great, russia and europe have had different interpretations and applications of the concept of modernisation. a material approach versus a more comprehensive interpretation, both material and immaterial. this was reflected again at the last eu-russia summit in rostov-on-don earlier this month. after signing a partnership for modernisation, president medvedev indicated that it was meant primarily in a technocratic sense. the european institutions therefore have the important task of continuing to appeal to the russian leadership for the necessary intangible aspects of genuine social modernisation, the safeguarding of basic fundamental rights in a free civil society. worryingly, the new powers of the russian fsb security service point the other, the wrong way, criticized the presidential human rights observer vladimir lukin. he rightly warns of the boomerang effect on the institutions of the state itself. his position deserves our full support, because the putin model must not be replicated in the region or in ukraine.
in writing. - today, in the vote on the bauer report, i voted against the commission's proposal for a directive on working time in road transport. this would bring the selfemployed within the scope of the directive. i'm aware that the traditional self-driver values being your own boss. in my view, however, the only way to force the commission to come forward quickly with a proposal to deal with the allegedly self-evident is to vote against it. these pseudo-independent drivers pose a direct threat to the drivers in the workforce. after this fundamental problem has been solved, a rapid review of what is best for the selfemployed is necessary.
in writing. - truck drivers may be employees, self-employed or quasi-self-employed. the latter are actually workers and should be given the same rights. the ep therefore asked the european commission in 2005 to clarify the situation through a legislative initiative. the present system of working time in road transport does not sufficiently regulate the legal position of the selfemployed and does not remedy any possible shortcomings. that is why i voted against this commission proposal, with the clear demand for a new and better regulation. the situation of genuine selfemployed drivers is, of course, different from that of employees and must remain so. i would therefore like the new regulation which we are asking the european commission to introduce to make the distinction between selfemployed and employed persons fair. there must be a proper regulation of working time in road transport which protects workers, abolishes the appearance of self-employment, gives rights to self-employed workers and which, together with the regulation of driving and rest times, ensures a safe situation on the road.
in writing. - a healthy diet is important. but how do you make a choice in that? reliable and comprehensible information on the packaging plays a major role. this new directive gives consumers better and clearer information, the main information appearing on the front of the pack and a more detailed description on the back. this allows the consumer to compare at a glance and, if he wishes, to choose a healthier product. a colour system indicating on the front whether the food was high or low in salt or high or low in fat was agreed by parliament. i voted for this system because it provides clarity to the consumer without being too informative. the original stoplight system, which indicated by red and green whether the product was unhealthy or healthy, was not put to the vote, as it had already been rejected. i found this system too simplistic and somewhat boring. the opportunity lost in this new system is not only the adjustment of the colour system but also the exclusion of alcohol. it is a pity that parliament did not vote for clear information on alcohol, such as the amount of calories, sugar and other additives. this new system will allow for healthier food if the consumer wants it.
in writing. - the key here is that consumers have a right to know what is in their food. they should be able to make an informed choice on the basis of clear information. the focus should be on clarity for consumers and feasibility for businesses. but more information is not always better! take for example the 'country of origin' labelling of products. this national approach is purely protectionist and therefore damaging, in particular, to european and dutch exporting companies. it sends out the wrong signal and actually an old-fashioned signal. a country of origin actually provides little information to the consumer and creates high costs for producers. i therefore regret that this house has chosen this part.

president. - our thoughts are with the victims of all the floods in central europe, but also with those in france today. these floods are another painful demonstration of the power that rivers can exert on us. we want to prevent flooding, and that is why we must look to the future. how can we prevent them? because we also know that climate change is causing rivers to process more water more often in less time. that means we have to look much more closely at how we can give space to our rivers and how we can prevent this kind of disaster in europe. because prevention is better than cure. europe really has a big role to play in this. first of all, countries need to cooperate much more across borders. if one country does something about its river area and another country doesn't, we're not getting anywhere. we need cooperation between countries in dealing with rivers. secondly, the eu must also take responsibility for its own funds in the future. too often now, eu structural funds are going to projects that only increase the risk of flooding. we must ensure that future eu projects that we finance do not increase these kinds of risks. that's an important assignment. finally, we as the eu must also explore how we can better use the natural buffer capacity of rivers to prevent these floods. concrete and more concrete only increases the risk of more flooding. so yes, we need space for rivers, and we need to use the natural buffer capacity. it's the only way to prevent future flooding. that must be our main objective for the future. prevention is better than cure.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. - i would like to thank the rapporteur for his good work and for the adoption of some of the recommendations of the envi committee. as rapporteur for an opinion, i would like to make a few comments, because in the future there will probably be more and more aquaculture and more and more pressure on people, animals and the environment. so we have to take very strict measures to protect them. many forms of aquaculture still have negative impacts, such as habitat destruction, use of chemicals, depletion and salinization of drinking water and agricultural land, disruption of biodiversity through fish and farmed fish escaping, and the displacement of indigenous populations in third countries from their habitats for industrial aquaculture. we need regulations on this. a sustainable certification system for aquaculture products is needed, certifying, inter alia, environmental and social impacts, food safety, animal welfare and economic and financial issues. i may sound a little negative, but i'm sure there's a future for aquaculture. we just have to make sure that this becomes a sustainable and animal-friendly aquaculture.
president, this week we are talking in this house and also in the european council about eu 2020: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. i think the milana report fits in perfectly. we consume a total of 5 million tonnes of aquaculture products in the union, and only 1 million tonnes come from the union itself. there are opportunities in that. i note from the words of the colleagues, particularly from scotland, that there is a great deal of experience. can i add an experience from the netherlands? we have the blue port project in the delta region of zeeland, in the eastern scheldt. we're working on the zeeuw tongue there. it is an excellent example of research in which a lot of investment is made, technological development and it remains within the standards of ecology and public health. there is a clear and sustainable cycle: the fish produce manure, the manure feeds the algae, the coral and the lamb's ear. this then becomes food for shellfish and sawfish, and those sawfish are used as food for the tongue. that's how you get a sustainable, closed loop and new business. this, if done right, is economically very important for coastal regions. europe had a great deal of knowledge, but that basic knowledge is not being shared fast enough, and i therefore call for intensive cooperation between the new initiatives, research, research and development. we have programs for that. i am not referring only to the fisheries fund, but also to the regional programmes and to decentralized cofinancing. i repeat, i support it wholeheartedly. there's a chance of a catch-up. one more thing: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets.
i am grateful to mr romeva i rueda for his excellent report. this report is important. as we say in dutch, "measuring is knowing", that provides the basis for further decision. but let's not fool ourselves politically. we've known what to do for a long time. we're going to have to temporarily stop fishing for bluefin tuna. i was personally present at the cites debacle in march. a divided european union has had no influence on the final result. the world, led by japan, has decided not to ban trade in bluefin tuna. the argument is: this is not going to be within cites, this is going to be within iccat. we'll have that chance in paris in november. my question to the commission is: i would like to ask the commissioner whether it is the intention of europe - and i would like to ask him to do so - to introduce a temporary ban on fishing, a quota of zero, in the framework of the iccat? and is the commission prepared to take that position in the very near future? we are not going to make the same mistake as we did with cites, which is to take a stand at the very last moment and then not be able to convince the rest of the world of our stand. so a very specific question to the commissioner: when will you make your statement to the iccat meeting? will that be a zero quota and will you then also start a strong lobby within the world to get that zero quota done?

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's maritime transport sector is not left behind. fortunately, data exchange on the ship's cargo is becoming much easier. at the same time, the committee on transport has made a few points in the debate. as regards inland waterway transport, for example, we have agreed that the european commission will report on whether the simplified procedures can also be applied to that sector, and i hope that the european commission will eventually reach that conclusion and the corresponding legislative proposals. as regards the exemption from piloting, it is hoped that a common framework will be established which will make competition between piloting services even fairer and at the same time continue to guarantee the quality of the piloting. mr sterckx has already mentioned it, the committee on transport also wanted to see a single language and the member states have promised a formula to make communication more common. i think that is a first step, but too modest. as long as there are still casualties and even deaths because those involved in a shipwreck sometimes speak three or more languages and do not understand each other, i will continue to work, mr president, to ensure that the common language between maritime and inland navigation, and also inland navigation, is english. this is a real step forward in terms of safety.
the report on the sustainable future of transport discusses the importance of the transport sector for the european economy and the completion of the internal market. this sounds promising at first, but unfortunately the report turns into a list of many left-wing hobbies: environmental protection, carbon reduction, improvement of social conditions, improved working conditions, you can't think of it that crazy or it's being quoted. of course, all this must be directed from europe. consumers and businesses will be burdened with high costs to meet the latest european requirements and these transport plans will ultimately be funded by european subsidies. these ambitions are destroying european economies. even before the current recession, europe was unable to keep up with the growth rates of the united states, let alone emerging economies like india and china. now, after the failure of the copenhagen climate conference, europe is making a desperate unilateral attempt to reduce the use of fossil fuels. the commission notes that the airport services agreement with ryanair was signed on 27 june 2013 and that the agreement was signed on 27 june 2013. how do they intend to achieve this in practice? are electric planes coming soon? although this report aims at financial and social prosperity in europe, it seems to be heading towards an increasingly weaker economic position of the european union with less employment and lower prosperity as a result. europe will never achieve what it wants. mr president, let us concentrate on the crux of the matter here, and that is an economically viable future for transport. if member states want to add policy to that, that is their own business, but please do not impose a european planned economy.

the rapporteur. - mr president, i would also like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs and colleagues present and absent for the really great cooperation. the aim of the new food regulation should, in my view, be broader than purely food safety aspects, because in today's society other aspects also play an important role for the consumer. think of environmental aspects, animal welfare aspects and ethical concerns. we are talking here about food that has been tampered with. innovation is fine, but not at the expense of human life and health. fortunately, a large majority of parliament agrees with me on this and i hope that we can also persuade the council to do the same. what are novel foods? well, these are products that were not on the eu market before 15 may 1997 because, for example, they had not been invented yet or because we in the eu did not yet know them as food. one example of this is nanotechnology. the producers are keen to bring this to the market. for consumers, innovation is always a great pleasure, and new technologies can also help us to combat obesity, for example. on the other hand, we must be very careful about allowing new technologies unless we are sure that these new technologies are not harmful to health, the environment or animal welfare. one of my principles is that when a new food is introduced on the market, the precautionary principle must always apply. no safety is guaranteed, not on our board. the consumer must also always have a choice, so if it is a novel food, state it on the label. now for the stumbling block: meat of cloned animals and their offspring. research has shown that consumers are increasingly spending money on animal products that have been produced in a way that is animal-friendly. cloned animals are often mutilated or have diseases. most of them are not even born alive, so animal kindness is a long way off. in the eu, cloning animals for human consumption is not yet a commercial practice. however, the possibility that imported clone meat is already on our plate cannot be ruled out. there are no specific eu rules on cloned meat, nor on the marketing of cloned seeds. the debate around cloned meat is not new. the european parliament has been debating animal cloning since 2006. in 2008, it adopted a resolution by neil parish to regulate the trade in cloned meat. however, in discussions with the council on this report it became clear that parliament's call would not be heeded. the council considered that meat from cloned animals should be covered by the new food rules. parliament therefore failed to reach agreement with the council at second reading. if parliament were to agree to cloned meat as part of the new food regulation, parliament would indirectly agree to cloned meat being on your plate. the new food regulation will also focus on safety for consumption and much less on animal welfare and ethical aspects. i therefore urge that separate regulations be made on the cloning of animals. time is of the essence, for the united states has just released the clone meat and clone seed trade a few months ago. without a european position, there is a high probability that cloned meat or cloned seeds can enter the eu market unnoticed and unhindered. i can only hope that parliament will be as firm on this point as it has been over the last three years. we must not come up with unpleasant surprises at the last minute, because it seems to me as a politician that it is very difficult to explain to the voters why we are creating cloned meat on the table of our consumers without a public debate on this.
the rapporteur. - mr president, i would like to thank all those who took part in the debate. i am very sorry that the council is not here, because i think that once again parliament has taken a very clear position on cloning and we actually think that it does not belong in this regulation, but that there should at least be an open public debate on it. i am not in favour of meat from cloned animals on my plate, but i can imagine others saying we want separate regulations. in any case, it is quite clear that we want separate regulations on this and that this should not be regulated in the regulation on novel foods. i hope that message is very clear, because we've talked here today about nanotechnology and about cloning. i have heard a lot about that, but the regulation on novel foods is about much more. it is about all those new techniques that are about to be discovered or that science is working on at the moment. we do not want to stand in the way of innovation, but we do want the consumer and the producer to know what they are up against and we want to make sure that the food that comes on our plate is also safe. i hope, therefore, that this debate on cloning will no longer stand in the way of the new food regulation and that in future developing countries who want to bring natural traditional foods onto the european market will not be deterred by this kind of confusion from trading with the european union in the case of ordinary natural products. i hope that tomorrow we in parliament will send the signal in the vote as clearly as we did in the debate today and i think that it will then be all right.

mr president, in a few months a great deal has changed in the structure of the european external action service. in january, at the hearings for the new european commission, my group and i criticised the fact that the commissioner for development cooperation was to become a sort of manager for only part of the policy and without any responsibility of his own. the danger then was that we would return to subordinating development policy to geopolitical goals and commercial interests and to the cynicism with which dictators have been kept in the saddle for decades by europe at the expense of their own people. now the structure has been greatly improved. i would like to pay tribute to the rapporteurs. development policy is of course integrated into foreign policy under the supervision and coordination of catherine ashton, but with a clear responsibility for the strategy, design and financing of policy. in this way, the eu can play the role needed to combat poverty and protect the rights of citizens in developing countries. our group will be keen to see that the eu takes on that role more strongly, and this foreign service can do that.

in writing. the european parliament's pvda delegation is in favour of the agreement, which will put an end to the mass transfer of european citizens' data in the foreseeable future. it is agreed that europe will develop its own system for collecting and retrieving banking data. this provides the opportunity to individualise the transfer. we keep data in our own hands and that's the best way to protect the rights of our citizens. the european commission will come forward with a proposal within a year. in three years' time, we should be able to introduce our own system. from that point on, there will be reciprocity. a european supervisor will monitor on a daily basis in the us what happens to the european banking data received. europol will be responsible for monitoring and approving the us requests. under pressure from the european parliament, the agreement already reached was reopened. we have not got our way 100%, but we have achieved that the americans cannot just snoop around in our data. the new agreement strikes a balance between protecting privacy and guaranteeing security. in the meantime, the fight against terrorism remains of the utmost importance.

the freedom of expression is under great pressure in europe and in the netherlands. under pressure from the multiculturalist elite, people are persecuted who point out, quite rightly, the dangers of religions and ideologies. criticism of religions and ideologies is always legitimate. the fact that one chooses to believe something is a personal, voluntary choice that should not be a burden to others. you're not born into a faith or an ideology, but you embrace it because you think it's your truth. but that's a personal truth, one you should keep to yourself and not bother others with. the moment that truth pretends to be an all-embracing truth, which also wants to change the law of the state, the criminal law, the civil law and so on, it becomes life-threatening. criticism of one's ideology or beliefs must be completely free from persecution in any form. individual decisions, individual experiences, must not be above the law. to offend or to hurt is a right that everyone possesses. that is not always pleasant for the recipient, but it is the basis of freedom of expression, because caresses and praises do not need protection. hurt and insult is allowed. the truth isn't always fun. freedom of speech was ultimately created as a protection of the citizen against tyrannical rulers who oppressed their citizens. the function of this freedom is to criticize and correct the ruling power. it is the ability of the ordinary citizen to express himself in the face of a social elite that wants to impose its explicit norms and values on citizens. in the netherlands, politicians, cartoonists and columnists are arrested, questioned or prosecuted precisely because they exercise this right, the right to express their opinion. the most important example of this is the madam president, thank you, commissioner, for your introduction. i know your personal commitment to freedom and democracy, so i am sure that our press freedom is in good hands with you. however, you say that the european commission has no general powers to protect freedom of the press, but i would say that that is a rather narrow interpretation of your powers, because first of all, protecting democracy and freedom and civil liberties is enshrined in the treaties, and you are the custodian of the treaties. secondly, if we as a european union demand from candidate member states that they meet certain standards, then we may assume that the commission will protect those standards within the european union as well. another element is that media ownership is increasingly a cross-border issue, and we could even imagine that there are non-eu companies who own european media. would you still say that if, for example, russian or chinese owners interfere with european media, it is not our business? i do not think so. the second issue at stake here is whether we should name and shame, whether we should single out individual member states. yes, i think we should. it may be unpleasant, and i would certainly not like my member state to be criticised, but if we are no longer capable of self-criticism, then how can we protect fundamental rights? i would like to remind mr busuttil, my esteemed colleague, that this parliament, in a previous mandate, adopted a resolution on the berlusconi government and freedom of the media that went way beyond the one that was rejected last year. so we do have a precedent. this house has repeatedly called for legislation – specific legislation – to give the european commission the tools to intervene and protect freedom throughout the european union.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right to vote. we have recently used the possibility of registering our partnership in the netherlands. that means that in the netherlands we get exactly the same rights as married heterosexual couples. we could have also chosen marriage, because in the netherlands marriage is open to same-sex couples. now, let's say i decide to go to work in poland and my partner comes with me. in that case, we will no longer be considered a couple, because poland does not yet recognize same-sex partnerships. in other words, by making use of the eu right of free movement of workers, we lose a number of essential rights, for example in the field of social security or pensions. so while married heterosexual couples retain their status, same-sex couples do not. the right to free movement is thereby restricted. as gue/ngl we do not advocate that the commission should come forward with proposals for the harmonization of family law in this area. that remains a matter for the member states. but we do ask the commission to come up with proposals to ensure that the rights enjoyed by workers under the free movement of persons, or by the selfemployed, are the same for everyone. the commission seems at present to be hesitant, to say the least, to develop proposals in this direction. that is why i and other colleagues have asked for the debate to be held this evening. i very much hope that the commissioner can promise that this will be done quickly and that, for example, in the area of social security and pensions, the rights of couples who move to another member state will be the same, regardless of whether it is same-sex couples or heterosexual couples.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union is able to meet the needs of its citizens. it is unacceptable that a large group of people should be excluded from this basic value. people who are married or in a same-sex partnership, but whose relationship is not recognised in some eu countries. this is not a technical problem. it touches the most important things in a person's life. can i take my sweetheart with me when i go to work in another country, and my children? my love will get a pension or a benefit if i suddenly go out of business. and it goes beyond that. mrs. reding, imagine you've been happily married to a woman for years. you're going to italy or greece together because you found your life's work there. for a few years, everything goes very well, but then fate strikes. your beloved partner gets in a car accident and ends up in intensive care. then it is only possible that you cannot make decisions about your life partner, that you cannot even be with her to hold her hand, because for italy you are nothing, you have nothing to do with her. i am very pleased, mr president, that more and more countries are opening up marriage or partnership to same-sex couples, and tens of thousands of people have already taken advantage of this. their numbers are growing. unfortunately, a number of member states are lagging behind. i am sorry, but we cannot force this from parliament. what we can ask for is recognition, so that the free movement of eu citizens is really possible for everyone in the eu. i would like to hear from mrs reding how she intends to achieve this with us.
author. − madam president, commissioner, just a couple of days ago i was watching a documentary on bbc world about some countries in the caucasus where it is customary for men to ride out and steal a bride. when their eye catches a nice girl they abduct her, take her home, rape her and then she is the man's wife. of course the families of the girls vigorously protest against this, because they feel it is not up to the men to decide to take the girl – it is up to her father to decide to give her away. it was a heartbreaking documentary. it was shocking, and why? because we feel that the choice of a partner, the choice of a husband or a wife is the most personal and intimate choice you can make in life. it is not for the man, not for your father, not for your brother – and certainly not for the state – to determine who will or will not be your partner. we have seen in history, and we still see today, countries where the state bans marriage between black and white people. in my country, not very long ago – this was something my grandparents were confronted with – catholics were not allowed to marry protestants even if they loved each other. there are still conservative muslims who feel their daughters should not marry non-muslims. there are many examples like that and we feel that is very shocking. but we still have countries in the european union that ban marriage between consenting adults of the same sex. i know that for some people it is very shocking that people of the same sex can love each other, but that is not really relevant. what is relevant here is that every single eu citizen should have the same rights. it is not for the european union or for the member state governments to judge a personal relationship. the european union has no competence in family law, but as michael cashman has just pointed out there are five countries already which have opened their marriage laws to same-sex couples. there are a number of other countries which have some form of recognised partnership. the very least we should be doing in the european union is apply the principle of mutual recognition. we do it for jam and wine and beer: why do we not do it for marriage and for relationships? i would like to ask the commission to take the initiative for mutual recognition between those member states which already have marriage or some form of registered partnership and give us a road map on how we will get to a situation where those relationships will be recognised everywhere.
president, cohabitation contracts and marriages of same-sex partners are not legally recognised in all host member states. this leads to multiple discrimination in family matters such as guardianship and inheritance, but also in tax matters and social rights, as if marriages and partnerships were less real or less important in this case. the proposed action plan in the stockholm programme does not include any proposal to solve this. there are no specific and concrete new initiatives for lgbt rights. but you have just said some fine words, because you said you were going to take action. i'm very happy about that. you spoke of concrete steps, well, concrete steps, and that it had to be very cautious, very careful to deal with certain member states that were having problems. my question is, firstly, will this happen during your term of office? my second question is, do these kinds of difficult situations also apply to other forms of discrimination, such as race, disability, age? i think that courage is necessary on all grounds, because all grounds are equal and discrimination on all grounds must be fought properly at the same time. let me make a suggestion. you said that you expected a report from the fundamental rights agency in november, including on discrimination in the field of homophobia. i would ask you to report to parliament on the basis of this report, which is supposed to give a reality check of the situation, on how you assess the situation and what concrete action you intend to take, preferably in giant steps.

on behalf of the ppe group president, on behalf of the epp group, i would like to say that we are very pleased with the agreement reached on european banking supervision. that's a major milestone. a number of member states initially put their backs into the sand, but our rapporteurs stood their ground and thanks to a strong commissioner and a decisive belgian presidency we were able to achieve this result. the introduction of the european semester is another good result. both are important steps to strengthen economic governance in europe. you also discussed in ecofin the various forms of bank taxation, since banks have to share in the costs of a crisis. you are right to try to do so in the g20, but progress is slow. the epp therefore agrees with the conclusion that if this does not succeed internationally, we in europe can take the lead, no, we must take the lead, in introducing a bank tax, a bank levy. but it must be done in a coordinated way, because member states are already introducing such a levy and that is a threat to our internal market. so, commissioner barnier, i would encourage you to make generous use of your right of initiative to get a coordinated banking levy introduced in europe according to the same criteria, and as with european supervision, you will find this parliament at your side. as regards the financial transaction tax, that is an issue which you should work very hard to get on the agenda in the g20 much more forcefully. i would like to warn against a europe of the single market. i know that mr schulz wants to introduce such a transaction tax in europe, but the disadvantages outweigh the benefits, because it is a global system. transactions will bypass europe as a whole. that 's why: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european banking system.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i want to be clear that indeed it is permissible to defend yourself; other countries are able to defend themselves; so arms exports are not off-limits. that is the position of the alde group. however, we should not export to regions of tension or of war. we should limit ourselves, and if we do not have a real, legally-binding regime on arms exports, it means that the 27 countries of the european union will not have a level playing field. they will compete and fight for orders and there will be no real consultation. so i think that the only way to resolve this issue is to have a binding common regime and i also support the words of my colleague mr brok on this matter.

and we are here on behalf of the european citizen - has overwhelmingly supported my call for a ban on cloned meat. so it is not about what the commissioner is now proposing, namely that the citizen can only recognise it when it is on the board. now clone meat has finally entered that food chain. i do not hear the commissioner say anything about how he is going to prevent that from now on and all i hear is that we have to wait again until sometime in november. i'm sick of waiting around. commissioner dalli, i have a question for you too: i am sure you will not be fooled by commissioner de gucht's behind-the-scenes announcement that the ban on cloning animals in the food chain, which parliament has called for, will lead to a trade war with the united states, brazil and argentina. i'd rather have a trade war than a war that's gonna cause unnecessary bloodshed. cloning is unethical and very, very animal-friendly. cloning results in animals being born dead on a large scale or having to be killed because they are deformed. while the commission waits, unclear regulations are causing this practice to penetrate the european union. the commission must legislate immediately and lift the moratorium on products from cloned animals and their offspring, which parliament has called for, today. finally, i would like to call on commissioner de gucht to stop playing the puppy dog of the united states and the biotechnology industry based there. that commissioner has been appointed in the general interest of the european union and its citizens and as a member of the commission he should not take instructions from any government, institution, body or agency, and i am simply quoting the treaty on european union. is europe really so far gone that it is allowing the repercussions of the world trade organisation to influence our policies to the detriment of our citizens? if so, i am ashamed of the state of the european union at this moment.
the commission has already taken the initiative of the european parliament's committee on agriculture and the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection to discuss the future of the potato starch sector in europe. a future which has been so threatened by the financial crisis that this sector may be in danger of disappearing completely from our continent. and this while the sector can play an important role in the long term in achieving our own eu 2020 objective. as you know, commissioner, under the health check, payments to this sector have been partly linked and partly disconnected. this situation will end in 2012 with full decoupling. let me be clear: i am in favour of this process, but in the present situation it requires the right approach. in order to survive and compete with other starch sectors, the potato starch sector will have to tap into new markets after full decoupling and the potential for doing so is great, especially in the bio-based economy, which is one of the key priorities of the european union's 2020 strategy. think of plastic made from potato starch instead of oil, or think of chemical elements for industry. however, the financial crisis has jeopardised preparations for this transition and for the opening up of these new markets, as investment and innovation have unfortunately been forced to stand still. in 2012, the sector is therefore facing a hard landing, with research suggesting that 40% of the market in the european union could be lost. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. we will not only see this in my region, in the north of the netherlands, but we will also see it in countries such as denmark, germany, poland and france. that is why we are sounding the alarm tonight, commissioner, and asking the commission to ensure that the sector makes a soft landing rather than a hard landing. so my questions are: is the european commission prepared to introduce temporary measures, for example for a two-year period and budget-neutral, using unused potato starch funds or agricultural resources? for example, is the european commission prepared to set up a voluntary restructuring fund to support producers who voluntarily reduce their production? in conclusion, commissioner, and this is of course the main question behind this whole debate: does the european commission share our view that the potato starch sector can play an important role in a bio-based economy and that it is therefore worthwhile to help this sector temporarily and budget-neutral to make this transition?
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's agricultural policy is not undermined by the current crisis. in the north of the netherlands alone, thousands of jobs are at stake, in an economically vulnerable area with few other major employers. these are all people, in groningen and elsewhere, who want to live and work in their own region. a balanced distribution of work across all european regions is important, including the prosperous north-west europe. so we need a transitional arrangement. it is the eu's responsibility to ensure that. it doesn't have to cost extra. there is money left over from subsidies in recent years in the sector because the harvest was bad. the agricultural budget can also be adjusted. of course, the potato starch subsidy scheme must end in 2012. no one doubts that, and the industry agrees. but there must be a future for this industry because it is close to home in our countryside, finding new alternatives to petroleum products and therefore becoming indispensable.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. unfortunately, this has not been the case in the potato milk sector. if the plans are carried out in the present situation, a lot of companies will go bankrupt because prices are too low. the big question is: is that worth the price? i don't think he did. i think that the potato sector in europe is a special sector. great progress has been made in the field of biochemistry. all kinds of new products are being developed and i think we should not interrupt that process. the commission's answers do not give me much optimism. i would ask the commission to look at the situation again and to start an intensive dialogue with the sector. i think that the soft landing which has been requested by many others will have to happen, because i think that the potato sector as a special sector must survive. i think that the commission will therefore have to take special measures.

president barnier, commissioner, you have just said that when you took office you said that you would give the single market a more social face. i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. but now for the real thing. yesterday, during the demonstration of hundreds of postmen outside the building, a female dutch postman told me that she is 55 years old, has only worked for the post office for 20 years and therefore expects to be one of the first to be fired by tnt-post next year. and she's not alone. at tnt-post in the netherlands all the postmen are being laid off, and that only because tnt is now dealing with competitors, notably its own subsidiary, where temporary flex workers deliver the mail for much lower salaries. we are talking about 15,000 redundancies of professionals, of postmen who are reliable and who handle our mail in this way and who have often done this work for decades. why, commissioner, have you never had the social consequences of the liberalization of the postal market investigated? or rather, why are you withholding from the european parliament the study which you have already funded and which we can read on the internet at pique.at. for everyone who sends or receives mail, liberalization is taking a dramatic turn. in the netherlands you see that mail is being delivered by different companies several times a day or in the middle of the night and on some days not at all, the closure of post offices, of all post offices, and the latest idea that is being developed within your services, within that group of regulators that you mentioned but that you have not consulted parliament about, to introduce extra high tariffs for people who live on islands or in the mountains. commissioner, can you now finally send us a full study of all the consequences of liberalization for post offices and for the service as such, and can you immediately put forward a proposal to postpone the date of entry into force of liberalization, a moratorium, until we have that data. that would give the single market a social face.

author. − madam president, kenya, as well as other african countries, is an essential partner of the eu in a number of key areas such as security, development and trade. africa played a crucial role in the establishment of the icc and its commitment to the court’s statute is also reinforced in article 4 of the constitutive act of the african union. the cotonou agreement between the eu and kenya lays down the conditions for partnership, and in this agreement conditionality on trade and aid in terms of adhering to democratic and human rights standards are clearly spelled out. unfortunately, on 27 august, omar al-bashir visited kenya as an official guest of the government, a dignitary. this happened during the period of an arrest warrant brought against al-bashir for his alleged role in the genocide in darfur in sudan. al-bashir can still defend his case before the icc, but first needs to be brought to the hague. kenya had the obligation to arrest him while he was on its soil and failed to do so. this is a serious problem. it is a signal that the government does not play a credible role in adhering to agreements it has itself signed up to, and this should be a serious problem for the eu. we have to make sure that we adhere to our own standards in fighting impunity, and we need to fight impunity in africa to create more stability and democracy. i would like to ask the high representative to take serious diplomatic action against kenya and to remind its leaders of their commitment to the rome statute.
- a writer. - madam president, there is little reason to believe in progress. the horrors of war and persecution in the 20th century also force modesty in this century. but there is one area in which i think we can make progress, and that is international criminal law. it started with the yugoslav tribunal, the rwanda tribunal, and it led to the international criminal court. no law, spoken by victors of a war as could be said of nuremberg, but by an independent international court. the most serious crimes can be tried there if the state in which they are committed does not itself prosecute them, whether intentionally or not. it is in this way that the world's sense of justice can be restored after great atrocities, that justice can be done to victims and that reconciliation can be achieved. peace to the world. this court is a deterrent to future war criminals. impunity is a license for new violence and cannot and should not exist, especially for the immense atrocities of which omar al-bashir is accused. kenya has signed the rome statute. pacta sunt servanda, treaties must be respected or they will be eroded and therefore it is intolerable that al-bashir was allowed to celebrate democracy in kenya unhindered, while in sudan, in his own country, he has allowed such gross violations of human rights to be committed. let this be an appeal, as mr vergiat has done, to the countries which have not yet signed the rome statute. the united states first. what is barack obama waiting for? strengthening the international criminal court brings closer the world order we want, an order which does not leave war crimes unpunished, an order which brings closer peace and which is based on the rights of people everywhere in the world.

on behalf of the group of the european right i shall begin with a point of order. our shadow reporter, mrs foster, has just informed me that she cannot be here. she's arriving later tonight, so i'd like to take her time. first of all, i would like to thank the rapporteur very much for her excellent work. this regulation, and the quality of this regulation, is good for air traffic in europe and will improve air safety. but if we really want to learn from accidents, then the investigation must be completely free from interference, from interference in the airlines by consumer organizations, but above all from interference by judicial authorities. if public authorities can interfere with independent research, then that research is at risk in terms of independence. it is the butcher who has the final say in the selection of his own meat, and that should not be our aim. it would have been better if we had introduced the so-called canadian model. then there is a fully independent investigative authority which, on the basis of objective criteria, invites the judiciary to join the investigation if there is a crime. we have that system in the netherlands, and there is an excellent functioning independent research council. the council of inquiry also regularly gives the public authorities a very clear lesson. that is the best system and now the text we have agreed with the council provides the opportunity to introduce such an independent inquiry. but the package is a real step forward. the information system for passengers involved in accidents in particular has improved considerably, and that is why we will not abstain tomorrow, but support the rapporteur and vote in favour.
for the greens/efa group it is true that a humane asylum policy entails a policy of expulsion. when you're processed, you have to go somewhere else. but that's a little different from juggling people. and somehow europe has figured out that if a person cannot be deported to the country he or she came from, we just take the nearest country or the country that someone traveled through before they arrived in europe. that's what i call juggling people. it's gotten to the point where we're trying to stop people in libya. and we say: we wash our hands of innocence if libya wants to send people back to eritrea. people from other countries - and this is afghans being deported to pakistan - we are obviously trying to get off our game. i heard a colleague from the christian democrats say: "how do we make these readmission agreements more attractive to those other countries?" that doesn't seem like a humane way to treat people! it is not the way to achieve a good european asylum policy. and that is what we are all for. the fact that we are sending them to a country like turkey, for example, where a lot of asylum seekers are travelling, means that these people cannot start a new life because they are not in the place where they want to be and are not in the place where they are welcome. and there's no money to pay for that.
the united left group is opposed to the proposed readmission agreement with pakistan. it's the wrong instrument at the wrong time. according to the united nations, pakistan's infrastructure - and you have to think of roads, railways, bridges - has been so badly affected by the floods that it is back to where it was decades ago. millions of people in pakistan have fled the water or returned to find that nothing of what they once owned is left. and you would say that in these circumstances, europe is primarily concerned with helping people. i am therefore disappointed to see that we must decide at this very moment on a return and readmission agreement which should lead to europe being able to send back illegal and rejected asylum seekers to pakistan. is this an example of the community of values that the european union is boasting about? the agreement also contains insufficient guarantees on human rights. pakistan is not a stable country at the moment. my question to the commission and indeed to the council is therefore: what is your basis for confidence that pakistan, which has not ratified the geneva convention but is hosting a large number of refugees, will actually provide protection for refugees in the present circumstances? you would expect the agreement to include safeguards that would do justice to the responsibilities you take on as a state when you deport someone to another country. the only thing we have really heard about this is that the european commission will ensure that the member states comply with their obligations under the european convention on human rights and the geneva convention. but my question to the commissioner is: how will the commission do that? do you send officials to pakistan when people are put on a plane and how long will you continue to monitor the fate of such people? or do we not get a report from you until it's too late for those people? the agreement contains too few guarantees and comes at the wrong time. my group therefore says: get it off!
the floods in pakistan are the worst in 80 years and there seems to be no end in sight. millions of people have been affected. there are many homeless and there is a threat of famine. are we going to send people back on the same plane as the one we use to send humanitarian aid to pakistan? an agreement between the european union and the islamic republic of pakistan is irresponsible. we cannot turn a blind eye to the human rights situation in pakistan. this one leaves a lot to be desired. pakistan has not even signed the geneva convention on refugees. we must conclude agreements on good terms, such as demanding the signature of the geneva convention or other agreements protecting fundamental rights. i am therefore very interested in the agreements which the european union is negotiating with other countries. it is important that we have an understanding of this so that we can see to it that good agreements are made. what about the takeover agreement with turkey, for example? the negotiations are reportedly almost complete. the final text still faces some resistance from some member states. can the commissioner indicate where the negotiations are stalled? what is the european commission doing to bring this to a successful conclusion? and will the commission, when the agreement is concluded, finally take the next step? will it finally be possible for turkish businessmen and students to travel to europe without a visa? it is unheard of for the european union to have a visa-free policy with the most diverse countries, but not with one of its candidate countries.
mr president, we have before us the report on free trade and economic relations with turkey. since the establishment of the customs union with turkey in 1996, trade between turkey and european countries has grown considerably. the freedom party sees free trade as a good thing and we support the proposals to remove technical barriers to trade and bureaucratic barriers. free trade is in everyone's interest, both for european countries and for turkey. at the same time, it should be made clear that cooperation with turkey should be limited to trade relations. the freedom party is against turkey's accession to the european union. europe can trade with the whole world, with countries like america, israel, japan, etc., but that does not mean that these countries must also become members of the european union. free trade with turkey should not be seen as a stepping stone to turkish membership of the european union. turkey is a trading partner of the european union, and the freedom party has no objection to that. but deep european integration or turkey's membership of the european union is not.
as turkey's rapporteur for parliament, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on this report on the clarity of his proposals. i agree with him. the customs union has brought a lot of benefits to turkish and european businesses. but, as the commissioner has also said, this customs union is not yet complete and is also far from perfect in a number of respects. the opportunities and challenges for our trade relations with turkey are clear. the remaining trade and technical barriers must be removed quickly and further harmonization will also be possible as we move forward with the acquis. what we must do above all is also to fight against counterfeit products, because that is what is causing so many european brand manufacturers so much trouble. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud, and it is not the commission's intention to take action in this area. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the social economy. if we want the social economy, at least we have made some progress. the energy. we have common interests in that respect. so we have to go for it. then there's the visa traffic. i have too often seen people in turkey have problems because they are treated differently and i will bring one more case to the commissioner's attention. the full application of the additional protocol to the association agreement remains crucial for turkey. if it were to be ratified, a huge obstacle to further trade relations would be removed and both turkey and europe could benefit from it. it is good that we have this report now and we will bring up all the political questions and answers again in the next report that the committee on foreign affairs is going to make.
- i'm the reporter. − with this own-initiative report, the european parliament is sounding the alarm about our biodiversity, our species richness and our ecosystems. species are dying off faster than ever before: 30% of amphibians, over 40% of mammals, birds, butterflies and reptiles and over 50% of freshwater fish are threatened with extinction. that is unacceptable, of course, not only from a purely biological or ecological point of view. i also believe that we have a moral duty to leave the earth in such a state for our children and grandchildren that they can do something with it. finally, it is also in our economic interest to invest in biodiversity. healthy ecosystems provide things like clean water, carbon sequestration, better harvests and more raw materials, and so on. the degradation of these ecosystems costs us 50 billion euros a year worldwide, and that bill is only going up. we, the european union and the member states, have failed miserably in this area. our goal of halting the decline in biodiversity by 2010 is far from achieved. the key question now is how we will achieve the new target we have set for 2020. first of all, of course, this is a matter of political will, but we also make a number of concrete suggestions in my report. for example, the spirit of the box must be ended. environmental policy and budgetary policy are too much on their own right now. that has to change into an integrated approach. biodiversity is not just a matter of european nature policy, but should also be part of other policy areas such as fisheries, agriculture, regional policy and so on. the first steps have been taken, i am aware of that, but we must continue along this road. the reform of the common agricultural policy, for example, offers opportunities for this, for example through the reimbursement of social services or compensation for farmers who produce sustainably in or near natural areas. secondly, create win-win situations where economy and ecology can go hand in hand: this is also an opportunity for european green jobs. another point is to make natura 2000 sites more cooperative and interconnected. there is no longer any cross-border approach, and animals and plants do not respect national borders. and we ask the commission for more leadership and more clarity. the differences between member states in the implementation of natura 2000, for example, are very great. overall, we support the european commission's proposals and ambition to halt the loss of biodiversity by 2020. that is the absolute minimum level of ambition. but restoring biodiversity must also be part of our ambition. and of course, attention to biodiversity needs to be stepped up at the international level. to that end, europe must speak loudly and clearly and with one voice at the nagoya summit next month. however, in the case of new plans, impact assessments are of great importance, both for the ecological and for the socioeconomic consequences, not as a delaying technique, not as an excuse, but to create clarity. finally, we also call on the european commission to adopt new policies, for example on invasive alien species. the protection of one species may require the active management of another. without tackling the gray squirrel, the native red squirrel in britain has little chance of survival. and the extermination of thousands of wild hamsters in southern limburg proves ineffective when a single fox eats 300 a year. in short, ambition and realism are what we are expected to do in the years ahead. finally, i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for the good cooperation which has led to the unanimous adoption of this report in the committee on the environment, just as i would like to thank commissioner potočnik, who cannot be here today because he is in new york for the biodiversity. but i trust that mr de gucht, who has had the opportunity to hear this contribution in his native language, will give a true and fair account of it to his colleague.
i would also like to thank the rapporteur for her work on this report, which has a sad reason: the failure of our own european biodiversity strategy. this strategy, adopted in 2000, should have halted the loss of biodiversity. not only did it not work, it was a colossal failure. we have not even managed to slow down the rate of decline. we human locusts continue to devour our planet, and our hunger seems insatiable. - is nothing being done? of course you are. natura 2000 is a major success story in many member states. there have been some results, but they are far too few. this is evident from the steadily growing list of endangered species. there are many fine statements, for example from the european council. i am sure that tomorrow at the united nations general assembly another will be added. but when it comes down to it, you look the other way. if it hurts and if there are really choices to be made, then the interests of the fishermen are suddenly greater than the survival of many species of fish, then the farmer may expand at the expense of a vulnerable natural area, then the timber industry is more important than the preservation of a forest. authoritative studies tell us that these wrong choices cost 7% of annual gross national product in the long run: that's 14 billion euros, that's 12 zeros, every year! a bigger stick behind the door is hard to imagine. the next few months will give us a chance to show that we mean business. and not even when it comes to the new biodiversity strategy. no, when the new agricultural policy is adopted, when the new fisheries policy is adopted, when new trade agreements are concluded. it is in other areas that the fate of our animals and plants is decided. let those same ministers who make such fine statements at such moments keep their backs straight and show courage by making the right choices. otherwise the new biodiversity strategy will be just as much a paper tiger as the previous one. and one thing i know for sure: paper tigers will never go extinct unlike the real tiger. man is producing more than enough of them!
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment. biodiversity in europe and the world is declining. that is very worrying. biodiversity is the heart of god's creation and the natural capital of this world. biodiversity is the basis of our food and the raw materials we use and ensures a stable climate. so it's time to stop talking, here too, and really get together and take action. the commission, the member states and this parliament must speak with one voice to achieve the ambitious 2020 target of halting biodiversity loss plus a global vision that ensures ecosystems are protected, valued and restored.
i would like to thank mrs de lange, the president-in-office, for her report on european biodiversity. europe is not doing enough to halt the loss of biodiversity. that is a serious matter which deserves attention and rightly so, as the rapporteur, mr de lange, has said. as a parliament we support the commission in its efforts to halt the loss of biodiversity by at least 2020. but that does mean something, because otherwise we will have to conclude again in 2020 that the objective has not been achieved. that means a different agricultural policy, a different way of spending money on our fisheries policy, a different infrastructure policy and a different way of regional development. all these matters will be dealt with in the financial perspective for the coming period and i assume that mr de lange and mr van dalen, who have spoken such fine words about biodiversity, will also give substance to those words when we talk about the financial perspective. the point is that we as europe really need to strengthen our biodiversity policy and protect it. otherwise, this is just another empty shell.

president. - i am addressing myself to my colleagues stevenson and reul, who say that the environment committee has acted too quickly. let us face it, this disaster happened five months ago and it is not too soon to come from europe with a resolution. as the environment committee, we've been working on this for a long time. i would also like to thank the commission very much, because it has acted proactively and has admitted that the legislation on liability is not clear, and that there are simply gaps between european legislation and national legislation. that kind of thing isn't handled properly. until we know if that's been properly handled, we should just stop any new deep-sea drilling. that's not a kind of stop for the whole sector, that's just for new deep-sea drilling. that's not an overreaction, that's a very calm reaction. we also know that there is an ongoing investigation in the gulf of mexico into what went wrong. that needs to be cleared up, and until then: i've never seen anything like it.

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, as we discuss the european position at the nagoya summit, outside this house a hunt is being undertaken: a relentless hunt for resources, a hunt for oil and gas, minerals, timber, food and water, a hunt for resources that are all provided by nature. in this hunt, chinese investors want to construct a highway through the serengeti national park to exploit the resources of central africa. there are saudi investors putting billions of dollars on the table to build 6 000 kilometres of roads through the rainforests of the congo to start huge palm-oil plants. we all want to stop the loss of biodiversity, but in the real world this hunt for resources is frustrating our goals. that is reality. therefore, the only way to stop the loss of biodiversity is by radically changing our behaviour. we need biodiversity for food, shelter, medicines, clean air, water, and so on and so forth. we simply cannot survive without it. we are reaching a tipping point at which damage is irreversible and, even worse, accelerating. that is the sense of urgency of the nagoya summit and that is the sense of urgency i expect from the ministers and commissioners who attend. unfortunately, political declarations and resolutions will not be sufficient to reach this sense of urgency. we need much more pressure. for that reason i have started an online campaign, along with many colleagues from all over the world. i want all of the people to have their voice heard on this issue; because people care about this. go through facebook to stop biodiversity loss and support this campaign. i will, together with many colleagues, present the thousands of signatures to the decision-makers in nagoya, to let them know that people depend on their perseverance and persistence to make nagoya a success.
in two weeks' time, mr president, we shall all be going to nagoya, japan, to talk about biodiversity. i wonder if our host will be serving us bluefin tuna or whale there. but the big debate there will again be about whether we should come up with money first or whether we should talk about objectives first. developing countries prefer to see money on the table first and the eu prefers to talk about objectives first. but it is clear that we must stop the loss of biodiversity in any case. if we don't act, the estimated cost will be over four trillion dollars by 2050. so, inaction is not an option, and that is why i am disturbed that the new dutch government wants to cut one billion euros in development aid, one of the most necessary financial resources to combat biodiversity loss in developing countries. if the eu really wants to achieve anything in nagoya, it must take the lead and develop good policies on agriculture and fisheries itself, and not come up with proposals on these matters after nagoya. the reform of the agricultural policy must go beyond a sustainable agricultural policy and must be a strong one. i therefore call on the commission to translate what is agreed in nagoya into measurable, verifiable proposals and to develop a long-term vision so that in 2011 we do not have to say again that we have not been able to halt the loss of biodiversity.

president, commissioner, international conventions must be submitted to the european parliament for approval. as members of parliament we must exercise our veto as much as possible, but our work is now being made almost impossible. we must repeatedly ask the commission to clarify the state of play in the acta negotiations. we're always getting hit with words. first we were fed the argument that the negotiations were far from complete, now we hear from you that this is just an enforcement agreement, which does not change existing legislation in the eu and in the member states. but i ask you: what is the added value of acta? you say that there will be no harmonization of criminal law nor an indirect obligation to adopt a three-strikes policy. but we are here again to discuss the content of this draft convention and these very points. yet every day i receive messages from concerned citizens and businesses. nevertheless, there is a general consensus among the groups in the european parliament that this draft treaty raises questions about the fundamental rights of our citizens and travellers from third countries. so i ask you with great insistence: what are the specific benefits of adopting acta? we are not going to vote for the report. and now a very specific question, commissioner: when will acta be discussed again by the negotiating parties? who are the parties that disagree? why is this? all of them, commissioner. we have again understood that the last dots on the i have not yet been put. how can we agree to a treaty that is being negotiated in back rooms?
mr president, yesterday the commission adopted a strategy to mainstream the legally binding eu charter of fundamental rights. as liberals we proposed this, so this is music to my ears. today the commission posted information on acta after the end of the last negotiations three weeks ago. the site has a paragraph called ‘positive aspects of acta’, but there are also some questions that might be labelled otherwise. firstly, do trade agreements or law enforcement treaties – which i think acta really is – also fall under the mainstreaming of fundamental rights? will the commission do an assessment? secondly, is the commission willing to go back to the negotiating table if fundamental rights are at risk through acta? will the commission wait to finalise the negotiations to take the parliament’s resolution of early next year into real account? as far as i know, no country has put its initials on a single page of acta. when it comes to the internet, acta even breaks new ground, the statement reads. the preamble states that acta seeks ‘to promote cooperation between service providers and rights holders’. article 2.18.3 goes on, ‘each party shall endeavour to promote cooperative efforts within the business community to effectively copyright infringements. this implies extra-judicial measures and challenges the division of power’. article 2.18.4 states that the competent authority, not necessarily judicial, should have the power ‘to order an online provider to disclose expeditiously to a rights holder information sufficient to identify a subscriber whose account was allegedly used for infringement’. besides pointing to the word ‘allegedly’, i would like to stress that internet service providers are concerned that they will have to start enforcing the law. so some room needs to be made under the heading ‘negative aspects of acta’ to assess the fundamental rights aspect that acta still has in the current draft and that the commission from now on is legally obliged to mainstream.

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i would like to thank the commissioner and the minister for their introductions. i very much welcome the very cooperative attitude of the commission and the council. we have had examples in the past where the cooperation was less intimate. i think that this example shows that, if the three main institutions can reach an agreement, we can speak with a single voice on behalf of 500 million citizens – and that is a very powerful voice. the resolution has been tabled jointly by six political groups, meaning that this parliament is sending a very strong political signal. i would also like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs of the other groups for their very good cooperation. there is one point which the commissioner and the minister did not address in their presentations: the issue of proportionality and necessity. both the council and the commission still claim that the massive – not ad hoc – collection and storage of pnr data is necessary and proportional in view of the fight against terrorism. i am willing to believe them, but this claim has to be substantiated: we need proof of necessity, proof of proportionality. why? the proportionality test is not a political test, it is legal test. european data protection laws require the collection and storage of data to be proportional and necessary. this is not something that we can agree on politically, it is something that has to be proven in court. if somebody goes to court and the court rules that these agreements are not watertight, then we look like idiots. the european parliament cannot be asked to endorse something which is open to legal challenge. that is a key issue. there are some other things which need clarification. i am pleased to hear that the commission is looking into the profiling issue, but i think that we need some further discussion on that. some of the member states are proposing a sunset clause. i would, of course, wholeheartedly endorse that; i hope that the council decides to introduce that. finally, the european commission refers to good relations with third countries, but these agreements cannot be seen as instruments of international diplomacy. they are instruments of international cooperation in law enforcement and protecting civil liberties and the rule of law. we need to get it right now, because we are not only negotiating with the us, canada and australia, but also setting a model and example for agreements with other countries.
the freedom party, mr president, is a strong supporter of measures that can help in the fight against terrorism, and islamic terrorism in particular. the pvv also attaches great importance to the protection of the privacy of dutch citizens and believes that where these interests conflict, careful consideration should be given. the u.s. government is allowed to have access to our passenger data, but under three conditions. the first condition is that they will be used only in the fight against terrorism. the second condition is reciprocity. american airlines must also provide this information to european authorities, in the same way as the other way round. this information should not be provided to a european agency but to the authorities of the european member states where the aircraft stop or have their final destination. the third condition is that the data transmitted must not be discriminatory. the united states and thus european countries can ask for any information provided on the basis of a voluntary choice made by the passengers. i emphasize that voluntary choice. data on religion, ideology, address, place of residence, telephone number, credit card number and data derived from the passport are data that may be provided. data which are not of the passenger's voluntary choice, such as sexuality, ethnic background and disabilities, should not be provided in my party's view. i would also like to stress once again, mr president, why these measures are necessary. let 's be clear . they're not needed to counter christian or buddhist terrorism. these measures are unfortunately necessary because islam is a threat to the free world. it is time for the members of this house to understand this.
mr president, we know that we are always a little behind society. things evolve, and politics and legislation follow later. the fact that there is now a format with civil rights requirements that a pnr agreement must comply with is very sensible, but it shows so well that we are always following the developments. the previous debate on the framework agreement on data protection also shows this. my fear is not that that list, which looks good, is now there and that you are using it. my concern is the maintenance of the various bilateral agreements between member states and other states. i would therefore ask the council, mrs turtelboom, whether it can guarantee that this is over once and for all. i would recommend that the negotiators take the european convention on human rights in book form and put the fundamental rights on the table next to you when you start negotiating, and look at them regularly.
