the commission's innovation strategy is clear, but this question remains open. it is clear that there is no need for new instruments, at least not entirely new ones, but better ones. long-term certainty is important, but we would prefer to have a well-functioning instrument with a vision for 2020. what is important is that innovation partnerships should be as concrete and clear as possible, with real impact, technology neutral and close to the market, and with a clear deadline. with the involvement of all key partners and industry, government, research institutions and, above all, the citizen, who is the driving force behind innovation and its success. it's very good that we're building on existing strength with healthy aging, actually living healthy rather than aging healthy, and it seems that actually aging healthy starts at conception. all partnerships should focus on the efficient and intelligent use of natural resources, starting with the most urgent problems: energy, the running out of raw materials. that's where more innovation is needed. it means the council's full commitment and enthusiasm. both are necessary to make the innovation strategy work. retreating beyond the borders is not an option. economics is really not just about the euro or the banking sector, the stability pact. it is about jobs in europe, a better position for europe in the world and if not through innovation, then how?

the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. cooperation between judicial authorities may be necessary to combat crime and to bring justice and legal certainty to the european internal market. but there is a danger that cooperation within the framework of the cfsp will lead europe to become involved in matters which fall within national competence. in paragraph 40 of the berlinguer report, the emphasis is on mutual recognition of marriage and family law, without any reference to article 81 (3) of the treaty on the functioning of the european union. it states that each member state retains sovereignty in the area of family law with cross-border implications. as this european principle has not been explicitly respected and as the ecr amendment on this point has been rejected, i did not support the report.

president, the european parliament is delaying the adoption of the 2011 budget. his claim to control the financing of the european union in the long term includes an eu tax, although this is not explicitly acknowledged. the european parliament thus bears the suspicion that it does not want to play a constructive role in the annual budgetary procedure, in accordance with article 234 of the treaty. this does not bode well for the financial outlook for 2014-10, which it is keen to be involved in. the european parliament has already achieved more than is good for europe, its citizens, its businesses and the budgets of the member states with a budget increase of 2.91%. we have a responsibility to make a reasonable budget for 2011. that can put an end to these perhaps still instructive budgetary perils.

author. − madam president, on 13 november, less than a week after the disputed national elections had been held, aung san suu kyi was released from house arrest after spending 15 of the last 21 years effectively imprisoned for her pro-democracy thoughts. as an iranian woman said in the movie the green wave, after being released from evin prison in tehran: ‘the moment i stepped outside i realised i had just moved from a small prison to a bigger one: that prison is called iran.’ people should not live in small or big prisons for their thoughts. the eu will see to and encourage further freedoms for all burmese citizens and remains concerned about aung san suu kyi’s well-being and safety, as she is being kept under surveillance by state security services. the welcome move of her release needs to be reflected in further expansions of freedoms to all citizens, which are systematically curbed through the 2008 constitution and the military junta. elections such as those which took place in burma this month, even if held in a climate of fear and repression, are often used by regimes to claim legitimacy and to suggest that a democracy is in place. however, any government derives its legitimacy from providing for the well-being of its citizens, and democracy is always more than ‘one man, one vote’. it is enshrined in democratic institutions, good governance, the rule of law and respect for human rights.

i would like to thank mrs mathieu, on behalf of the ppe group, for taking the lead on a matter which has been in play for many years. if i am correct, mrs malmström, there was a proposal for a directive on the conditions of residence for work in the european union for third country nationals as far back as 2001. and that proposal was withdrawn in 2006, and then in 2007 this proposal came up with this unique procedure for third-country nationals entering the eu to work. residence and work permits are then combined in one case. the proposal now before us - and i do not entirely agree with mr cercas - states that third country nationals holding a single permit have social rights which are in fact the same for everyone. the debate, and i thank mr cercas, because i like to debate with him, was very heated in the committee on social affairs on two points. first of all, the scope, where - and i agree with this - posted workers cannot be eligible for a single permit. this is described in article 3 (2) (b) of directive 96/71/ec, the posting of workers directive. i believe that the social position of thirdcountry nationals on secondment is and was adequately described in that directive. what i thought and think is important is that a level playing field is created, that loan workers are not allowed to be cheaper than regular workers. i wonder, however, whether not mentioning the secondment directive could not get us into trouble in the future. the second point, mr president, which i myself had great difficulty with and which has now been modified by my amendment, is the principle of breaking down the principle of equal treatment. i was and am of the opinion that the principle laid down in regulation (ec) no 883 must also be the basis for this single permit. i think that the compromise now reached by the council is a good compromise, mr president, because it guarantees equal rights and equal treatment.

mr president, it is clear that the lisbon treaty takes a different view of fundamental rights than we were used to. first of all, of course, by the accession of the european union to the echr, which created a strange situation of competing courts. it is still unclear exactly how that will develop. i am afraid that with the union's accession to the echr we have entered a european legal minefield. can the commissioner comment on that? the second point, mr president, is the incorporation of the charter of fundamental rights into european law. i have a specific question to the commissioner about a judgment handed down by the european court of justice on 9 november 2010. the cases in question are joined cases c 92/09 and c 93/09 volker and hartmut eifert. in short, these cases were about the right to privacy versus the right to transparency of european subsidies. in these judgments, the court has held that the right to privacy is above the general european interest in transparency. this statement surprises me very much and i would like to know what the european commissioner thinks about this case in the context of the incorporation of the fundamental rights of the charter into existing and future eu legislation.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). if the restraining order that her stalker has in the netherlands does not travel with her to spain, she will be a wild beast there, and her children will be too, if the spanish authorities do not recognize or enforce that same restraining order. spain does, however. this protection also applies to the freedom fighters. they must be able to speak in other european countries without the threat to them being in the audience. the european protection order is a wonderful initiative that has the support of the dutch government. with the new coalition , their position has unfortunately been turned 180 degrees: women, men and children who live in fear every day are being left behind. it just can't be that someone's safety stops at the border. for the sake of a hundred thousand people, i am now saying yes to the european protection order. i expect all governments, including those of my own country, to do everything possible to strengthen the security, protection and freedom of citizens so that all people can live in freedom and security.
i would like to thank mrs hedh and mrs bauer for their hard work as co-rapporteurs. this directive concerns the prosecution, prevention and protection of trafficking in human beings. the green group would like to see more robust action, particularly in the area of the protection of victims of trafficking in human beings. i am referring in particular to the right to reside in a country after being traded and perhaps rescued. how do we ensure that a victim can really build a new life? now, that is not sufficiently regulated in this directive; it is only a form of temporary residence permit, after which you do not know whether you will be returned to the place from which you were traded. we therefore hope that the directive will be revised to deal with these matters. we hope that we will then be able to provide real help to victims. another aspect that is heating up the minds of this house is whether or not to criminalize those who use the services of a trafficked person. the majority of the green group nevertheless remains of the opinion that if you make it a criminal offence, you are further embarrassing the trafficked person, because his or her activity becomes completely illegal. i am also glad to see that this directive leaves the member states free to choose, but i would ask any member state that joins in: how do we empower the victim?
the basis for many of the developments of our time was laid hundreds of years ago. i am thinking of the industrial revolution, the advent of representative democracy and the abolition of slavery. the current fight against human trafficking is a continuation of the fight that william wilberforce once started. unfortunately, human trafficking is one of the biggest hidden problems in the european union. it damages the fundamental rights of people and the foundations of our democracy. that is why i fully support the report before us. i understand, however, that the proposed standards of punishment for repeat offenders will unfortunately remain low. the current proposal does not call for a higher penalty standard if a trafficker is arrested more often. i'm sorry to hear that. i am in favour of an approach whereby a trader who is caught for a second or third offence should be liable to a life sentence.
in writing. − although i agreed with the majority of the gomes report, i have voted against it as it focuses too much on the eu coordinating civil-military cooperation. military competences should remain with the member states and not with the eu and the commission. i have no problem with member states cooperating on an equal level in the case of a cbrn event – and in the case of an environmental disaster resulting from such an incident, it is crucial – but i do have a huge problem with this report potentially opening the door for militarisation of the eu.

first of all, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr bendtsen, for the report he has drawn up, which sets out very well the essence of what needs to be done here. but let us put this in perspective. everyone knows that by 2050 europe must have reduced either 80%, or as we say 95%, of its greenhouse gases. that's an incredible task. and where's the biggest profit? the biggest gain is made by using even less energy. we have to import less, substitute less, develop fewer alternatives, and it is simply the cheapest! it is therefore absurd that we already know that europe is not going to achieve the energy saving objective and that we are focusing on achieving reduction targets and renewable energy, especially from the commission. but the goal of saving energy, of using less energy, we are not going to achieve that. and there's so much to learn from everything around us. for example, the built environment. this uses about 40 percent of our energy, 99 percent of which is existing buildings. they need to be renovated to be more energy efficient. the rate in europe right now is 1.4 percent a year, which means it will take us more than 60 years to replace that entire built environment. so we are in 2070 when we have targets for 2050! that 's the point . we must make sure that we are making much faster profits in, for example, the built environment. that is why an amendment has been tabled for a european building initiative. it saves everybody money, it's the cheaper options, we prepare for the future, and -- most importantly -- it saves people money. they have to spend less energy. and that's an argument i hear so little. it's the people who end up paying less for energy use. that's what it's supposed to be about!
commissioner, i would be very happy to comment on this report. it is an excellent report because it is a report which achieves several things at once: environmental objectives, co2 reduction, but also in terms of our production and the other objectives to remain competitive. it helps the environment and it helps the wallet. the commissioner also spoke of his ambition to come up with an action plan quickly. three points, though: the first concerns those national energy efficiency action plans. there is still a lot of room for improvement and the european commission must look at it critically. the commissioner indicated that he would work in two phases. the first stage is consultation with some urge and possibly in a second stage urge. i have also stressed this with the commissioner and in amendments, and i am glad that this is now being emphasized. i have only one question for the commissioner: do you have the tools to move on when you need to get to that urge phase? would you give an analysis of that, now or soon, or in your action plan? the second point concerns regional policy. mr swoboda has also spoken about this. we can do a lot with those funds. the money that is available is not even being used for energy efficiency in most countries. and that is why i have tabled an amendment to article 81 to clarify that text. it is not always about more money, it is also about making good use of existing resources. and a third point - and i agree with mr swoboda on this too - is that we must involve citizens, local authorities and businesses more in this energy saving, such as the labels, the agreement with the mayors, the agreements with industry. in my country, the netherlands, the agreement with industry alone has brought about 2% efficiency improvements per year. that's as concrete as it gets! thank you, good luck, and thanks to the rapporteur!
in this time of crisis, we must not lose sight of the fact that the euro has brought us a great deal of prosperity, employment and stability over the past decade. that is why the euro is worth more than defending vigorously. but that requires a decisive european summit and much, much more unanimity. that's too much to ask. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in this debate, mr president, the magic word seems to be eurobonds, as if it makes the problems of the national debt disappear like snow in the sun! but those in this house who are calling for eurobonds must also realise that this entails heavy obligations and far-reaching budgetary discipline which goes far beyond the proposals now before us to strengthen the stability pact. president trichet said "a fiscal union". are those in this house who are calling for eurobonds prepared to do so? i have my doubts about that. we must put our energies into the proposals now before us to strengthen the foundation under the euro. that is urgent and that is what we are working hard on in this house. a more rule-based approach, also in the preventive arm of the stability pact, because prevention is better than cure. and to make member states much more co-responsible, not only for the benefits but also for the obligations and the commitment that comes from the stability pact.

author. − mr president, the practice of caning – the hitting of a person with a cane – is a form of corporal punishment that is often applied in malaysia and this is a reason for concern, because tens of thousands of people are subject to this kind of punishment, particularly immigrants, and the list of offences that can lead to caning is growing. malaysia is thus violating its commitment to resolution 8/8 of the un human rights council, which states that corporal punishment can be tantamount to torture. the legal process leading to this form of punishment is often lacking and immigrants are not being informed about the charges against them, or are being denied the right of access to legal counsel. we welcome the outspoken call by the malaysian bar association – which represents 8 000 lawyers – for the abolition of caning. the european parliament calls on the malaysian authorities to enact a moratorium on caning and all other forms of corporal punishment, while working on the abolishment of this law in letter and in practice. the human rights commission of malaysia and the malaysian law reform committee should make adequate recommendations to the government concerning the abolition of corporal punishment laws.
author. − mr president, the so-called anti-homosexuality bill before the ugandan parliament provides for the punishment of homosexual acts by imprisonment for terms ranging from seven years to life, or even the death penalty. the european parliament strongly condemns this bill and, in so doing, we are not imposing european values on other states, as some have suggested in an attempt to avoid responsibility for human rights violations and responsibility for protecting citizens from being attacked on the basis of their sexual orientation. the real threat and the climate of fear were underlined in the october and november issues of rolling stone, a local newspaper, which listed the names and personal details of people alleged to be homosexual, inciting readers to harm or hang them. several people were consequently attacked. in fact, homosexuality is legal in only 13 african countries and it is a criminal offence in 38 others. in mauritania, sudan and northern nigeria it is punishable by death. the ugandan authorities are obliged, in accordance with the cotonou agreement, to observe international and human rights, and the commission, the council of the european union and the external action service should also make full use of the toolkit for promoting and protecting the enjoyment of human rights by all lgbt people.
author. − mr president, about 250 eritrean migrants are believed to be held hostage in the sinai desert and inhumane treatment and rape are reported while these people await a ransom that needs to be paid to human traffickers for their release. recent events are part of a network smuggling sub-saharan migrants through egypt to israel that has been operating in the sinai since 2007. the egyptian authorities are making welcome efforts to investigate the information provided by the unhcr. diplomatic procedures with the egyptian authorities should be stepped up by the un high commissioner for refugees to eliminate the trafficking networks and prevent people from being smuggled. at the same time, egypt should improve the treatment of refugees according to international standards. this means that, during the justified defence of borders, no excessive or even lethal force should be used against immigrants or refugees, and this advice is something that we might also give ourselves in the eu once in a while.
i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community. the resolutions as they stand are a weak copy of what we started with at the beginning of the week. we're basically saying now: "it is bad". and we point to the fact that eritrean refugees are being held hostage in the sinai. we are not prepared to talk about the links between italy and libya and the closing of the whole of europe to migrants, which is forcing political refugees to move elsewhere. they must now tread roads we must tread even more dangerous than before. anyone who leaves eritrea is automatically a political refugee, because you can't go back and your family is at risk. that we in this parliament are not prepared to say: if europe wants to shield itself from migrants if necessary, it will have to open its embassies around eritrea, so that people can easily apply for asylum. that was not possible in this resolution. and when i hear the ppe group talking about what is needed in egypt, i think: what do we have to do now and why do we not want to admit that people are at risk here because we do not want to take responsibility. we've been shutting people out, and in doing so, we've put lives at risk.
